one obtains from (6), for $A_3$, the constant value $1$. In order, however, to determine $A_2$, we must go one term further in the expansion. We put in
\[
 x_0^4+\gamma_3x_0-27=-A_2x_0^2
\]
for $x_0$ the expansion
\[
 x_0=-q^{-1/3}+6q^{1/3}+\cdots,
\]
and find $A_2=18$, so that the sought equation is
\begin{equation}
 x^4+18x^2+\gamma_3x-27=0.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}

If $n$ is odd and not divisible by $3$, take $c$ divisible by $12$. Then
\[
 \lambda\equiv n,
 \quad \alpha\equiv0,
 \quad \partial\equiv0,
 \quad \beta\equiv a\partial_2,
 \quad \gamma\equiv-a_2\alpha
 \pmod {12},
\]
and one obtains
\[
 r^2=(-1)^{(\beta-1)/2}=(-1)^{(n-1)/2}(-1)^{(a-a_2)/2},
\]
hence the theorem:

4. If $n$ is relatively prime to $6$ and $c$ divisible by $12$, then the quantities
\[
 P_{c,\partial,a}^{2}\gamma_2(\omega)^{n-1}\gamma_3(\omega)^{(n-1)/2}
\]
are roots of a multiplier equation of the first stage.

Since the functions $P^2$ become neither infinite nor zero for any finite value of $j(\omega)$, we conclude that the coefficients of the equation whose roots are the $P^2$ are integral rational functions of $\gamma_2,\gamma_3$, and that the last coefficient is a constant.

If $n$ is a prime number $p$, then the roots of this equation are
\[
 x=p\left({\eta(p\omega)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^2,
 \qquad
 x_h=(-1)^{(p-1)/2}e^{2\pi i h/p}\left({\eta\left({12h+\omega\over p}\right)
 \over\eta(\omega)}\right)^2,
\]
where $h=0,1,\ldots,p-1$, and the initial terms of the expansions are the following:
\[
 x=pq^{(p-1)/6}\cdots,
 \qquad
 x_h=(-1)^{(p-1)/2}e^{2\pi ih/p}q^{-(p-1)/6p}\cdots,
\]
from which the value $(-1)^{(p-1)/2}p$ is obtained for the last coefficient. From the fact that the first term in the expansion of $j(\omega)$ by powers of $q$ has coefficient $1$, one readily concludes that the numerical coefficients in these equations are rational integers.

For $p=5$ the equation in question has the form
\[
 x^6+A_1\gamma_2^2x^5+A_2\gamma_2x^4+A_3x^3+A_4\gamma_2^2x+5=0,
\]
where $A_1,A_2,A_3,A_4$ are integral rational functions of $j(\omega)$ and, as is easy to see, constants.

From the initial terms one obtains immediately
\[
 A_1=0,
 \qquad A_2=0,
 \qquad A_4=-1.
\]
But in order to determine $A_3$, one goes in the expansion of $x_0$ to the next following term:
\[
 x_0=q^{-7/15}(1-2q^{2/5}+\cdots),
\]
from which $A_3=10$ is obtained; thus, for $n=5$, the multiplier equation is
\begin{equation}
 x^6+10x^3-\gamma_2x+5=0.
\tag{8}
\end{equation}

In the same way the equations for $p=7$, $p=11$ are calculated, by using the expansions of $x_0$:
\[
 p=7:\quad x_0=-q^{-1/7}(1-2q^{2/7}-q^{4/7}+2q^{6/7}\cdots),
\]
\[
 p=11:\quad x_0=-q^{-5/33}(1-2q^{4/11}-q^{6/11}+2q^{8/11}+q^{10/11}+2q^{12/11}+\cdots),
\]
while from $\gamma_2(\omega),\gamma_3(\omega)$ only the first terms are ever needed. Thus one finds
\begin{align}
 p=7:\quad& x^8+7\cdot2x^6+7\cdot9x^4+7\cdot10x^2+\gamma_3x-7=0,
\tag{9}\\
 p=11:\quad& x^{12}-11\cdot90x^8+11\cdot40\gamma_2x^6+11\cdot15\gamma_3x^5
 +11\cdot2\gamma_2^2x^2+\gamma_2\gamma_3x-11=0.
\tag{10}
\end{align}

If $n$ is an odd square, take $c$ divisible by $8$. Then $a:e$ and $\partial:e$ are likewise squares. Further,
\begin{equation}
 \alpha\equiv\partial\equiv0\pmod8,
 \qquad
 \lambda\equiv1\pmod8,
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 a=\beta\partial_2,
 \quad a_2\equiv\beta\partial,
 \quad a\equiv\partial\equiv e\equiv1,
 \qquad
 c=\partial\hat\partial_2,
 \quad c_2=-\alpha\partial,
 \quad a_2\equiv\partial_2\equiv e_2\equiv1
 \pmod8.
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
The root of unity $r$ in (2) receives the value
\begin{equation}
 r=\left({\alpha\over\beta}\right)
 \ii^{(1-\beta)/2}e^{{2\pi i\over3}(\alpha+\partial)-(\partial-1)\alpha\gamma/8}.
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
But by (12) $\beta$ is divisible both by $e$ and by $e_2$, and $\beta e e_2$ is a square; hence
\[
 \left({\alpha\over\beta}\right)=\left({\alpha\over ee_2}\right)
 =\left({\alpha\over e}\right)
 \left({\alpha\over e_2}\right)
 =\left({\partial\over e}\right)
 \left({\alpha\over e_2}\right)
 \quad(\hbox{because }\alpha\partial\equiv1\pmod e).
\]
Further by (12)
\[
 \left({\partial\over e}\right)=\left({c\over e}\right)
 \left({\partial_2\over e}\right),
 \qquad
 \left({\alpha\over e_2}\right)=\left({c_2\over e_2}\right)
 \left({-\partial\over e_2}\right),
\]
and
\[
 \left({c_2\over e}\right)=\left({c_2\over e_2}\right),
 \qquad
 \left({-\partial\over e_2}\right)=\left({-e\over e_2}\right),
 \qquad
 \left({e_2\over e}\right)
 \left({-e\over e_2}\right)=(-1)^{(e+1)(e_2-1)/4};
\]
therefore
\[
 \left({\alpha\over\beta}\right)=
 (-1)^{(e+1)(e_2-1)/4}
 \left({c\over e}\right)\left({c_2\over e_2}\right),
\]
and
\[
 \ii^{(1-\beta)/2}=\ii^{(1-e)/2},
 \qquad
 \ii^{(a-a_2)/2}=\ii^{(e-e_2)/2}.
\]
By the interchanges (2), $P_{c,\partial,a}$ therefore goes into
\begin{equation}
 P_{c,\partial,a}\quad\hbox{under }(\omega,\omega+1)\hbox{ into }
 e^{{2\pi i\over8}{\lambda-1\over2}}P_{c_1,\partial,a},
 \quad
\hbox{and under }\left(\omega,-{1\over\omega}\right)\hbox{ into }
 e^{{2\pi i\over8}\{\beta(\alpha+\partial)-(\partial^2-1)\alpha\gamma\over4\}}P_{c_2,\partial_2,a_2}.
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
If $n$ is still not divisible by $3$, take $c$ divisible by $3$, whereby
\[
 \lambda\equiv1,
 \qquad
 \alpha\equiv\partial\equiv0\pmod3,
\]
and the third roots of unity occurring in (14) receive the value $1$.

From this the following theorems result:

5. If $n$ is an odd square and $c$ divisible by $8$, then the quantities
\[
 P_{c,\partial,a}^{3}
\]
and, if $n$ is an odd square not divisible by $3$ and $c$ is divisible by $24$, then the quantities
\[
 P_{c,\partial,a}
\]
are roots of multiplier equations of the first stage.

The first examples are $n=9$, $n=25$.

For the formation of these equations one can proceed in different ways. We shall here, following Kiepert,\footnote{\emph{Zur Transformationstheorie der elliptischen Funktionen}, Crelle's Journal, vols. 87, 88, 95.} take the path of expressing, by \S\,69, the roots of a transformation equation belonging to degree $p$ rationally through those for degree $p^2$, and then inserting this expression into the transformation equation belonging to $p$.

According to 3., formula (7), the equation
\begin{equation}
 x^4+18x^2+\gamma_3(\omega)x-27=0
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
is satisfied by the two functions
\begin{equation}
 -\left({\eta\left({\omega\over3}\right)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^6,
 \qquad
 27\left({\eta(3\omega)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^6.
\tag{16}
\end{equation}
If we denote the first of these values by $x$, then the second passes, under the substitution $(\omega,\omega/3)$, into $-27/x$, and therefore $x$ also satisfies the equation
\begin{equation}
 27^3+18\cdot27x^2-\gamma_3\left({\omega\over3}\right)x^3-x^4=0.
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
Now put
\begin{equation}
 y=\left({\eta\left({\omega\over9}\right)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^3.
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
Then $x$ passes under the substitution $(\omega,\omega/3)$ into $y^2/x$, so that one also obtains the equation
\begin{equation}
 y^8+18y^4x^2+\gamma_3\left({\omega\over3}\right)y^2x^3-27x^4=0,
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
and by eliminating $\gamma_3(\omega/3)$ from (17) and (19), after removing the factor $y^2-27$, one obtains
\begin{equation}
 x^4-18x^2y^2-y^2(y^4-27y^2+27^2)=0.
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
Solving this quadratic equation for $x^2$, one obtains
\begin{equation}
 x^2=y^3+9y^2+27y=(y+3)^3-27,
\tag{21}
\end{equation}
where the sign is decided by inserting the initial terms of the expansions, most simply since this equation is also satisfied, according to \S\,69, by
\[
 x=27\left({\eta(3\omega)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^6,
 \qquad
 y=27\left({\eta(9\omega)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^3,
\]
putting
\[
 x=27q+\cdots,
 \qquad
 y=27q^2+\cdots.
\]

If in (15) the first power of $x$ is isolated and the equation is squared, then insertion of (21) gives the sought multiplier equation for the ninth transformation degree. It receives a simpler form if one puts
\begin{equation}
 t=y+9,
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
namely
\begin{equation}
 [j(\omega)-27\cdot64](t-27)=(t^2-36t+27\cdot8)^2,
\tag{23}
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
 j(\omega)(t-27)=t(t-24)^3.
\tag{24}
\end{equation}

Quite similarly one can proceed for the twenty-fifth transformation degree.

If we put
\begin{equation}
 x=\left({\eta\left({\omega\over5}\right)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^2,
 \qquad
 y=\left({\eta\left({\omega\over25}\right)\over\eta(\omega)}\right),
\tag{25}
\end{equation}
then first, by 4., (8),
\begin{equation}
 x^6+10x^3-\gamma_2(\omega)x+5=0,
\tag{26}
\end{equation}
from which, as above, the two equations
\[
 5^5+10\cdot5^2x^3-\gamma_2\left({\omega\over5}\right)x^5+x^6=0,
\]
\[
 y^{11}+10y^6x^3-\gamma_2\left({\omega\over5}\right)y^2x^5+5x^6=0,
\]
and by eliminating $\gamma_2(\omega/5)$,
\[
 x^6-10x^3y^2(5+y^2)-y^2(y^8+5y^6+5^2y^4+5^3y^2+5^4)=0.
\]
Solving this equation for $x^3$ gives
\begin{equation}
 x^3=y^5+5y^4+15y^3+25y^2+25,
\tag{27}
\end{equation}
and if, for abbreviation, we denote the right hand side of this equation by $\chi(y)$, then from (26)
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_2(\omega)y^2=\chi(y)^2+10\chi(y)+5^2.
\tag{28}
\end{equation}
This is the sought equation of degree $30$ for $y$.


\clearpage
\section*{\S\,73. The Schlaefli modular equations.}

One obtains simpler transformation equations when to the domain of rationality, which until now consisted of the rational functions of $j(\omega)$, one adjoins the quantity $f(\omega)^{24}$. To this domain of rationality belong the functions of $\omega$ which remain unchanged by the two substitutions
\begin{equation}
 \left(\omega,-{1\over\omega}\right),
 \qquad
 (\omega,\omega+2).
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
If, therefore, a system of $\nu$ functions $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ is only permuted among itself by the substitutions (1), then these functions are the roots of a transformation equation whose coefficients depend rationally on $f(\omega)^{24}$. For an odd $n$, which we shall now always suppose, this includes, as we have already shown before by another method, certain powers of the quantities
\[
 P_{a,c,\partial},
\]
where, once for all, it is to be noted that $c$ is supposed divisible by $16$, and, if $n$ is not divisible by $3$, by $48$.

The somewhat extended principles of \S\,70 lead comparatively simply to the calculation of these equations.

An extension is necessary for the following reason. In the preceding considerations we could conclude that, if a rational function of $j(\omega)$ remains finite for every finite $\omega$ with positive imaginary part, then it is an integral function of $j(\omega)$, since to every finite $j(\omega)$ there also belongs a finite non-real $\omega$ (\S\,52). But for rational functions of $f(\omega)$ we can conclude from finiteness for every finite imaginary $\omega$ only that they are integral functions of $f(\omega)$ and $1/f(\omega)$, since only to every finite $f(\omega)$ except $f(\omega)=0$ does there belong a finite imaginary $\omega$.

To the substitution
\begin{equation}
 \omega\mapsto {\omega\over\omega+1}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
there corresponds, however, the interchange
\[
 f(\omega)\mapsto {2\over f(\omega)}\qquad[\S\,34,(13)].
\]
If, therefore, the functions $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ are so chosen that they too are only permuted among themselves by the substitution (2), then the coefficients of the equation whose roots they are depend rationally on
\[
 f(\omega)^{24}+{2^{24}\over f(\omega)^{24}}.
\]
They are integral functions of this combination if the $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ remain finite for every finite non-real $\omega$; and they are constant if all $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ remain finite also for $q=0$, that is, for $f(\omega)=0$ and $f(\omega)=\infty$. In this case all the $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ are equal to one and the same constant.

Thus the following theorem results. Form integral rational functions $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ from
\[
 f\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right),\quad
 {1\over f\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right)},
 \quad f(\omega),\quad {1\over f(\omega)},
\]
which have the properties:

1. to be only permuted among themselves by the substitutions
\[
 \left(\omega,-{1\over\omega}\right),
 \qquad (\omega,\omega+2),
 \qquad \left(\omega,{\omega\over\omega+1}\right);
\]

2. not to become infinite for $q=0$.

Then
\[
 \Phi_{a,c,\partial}=\hbox{constant}
\]
is a transformation equation.

In order to satisfy these conditions, one must first investigate the effect of the substitutions (1) on the functions $f$. This follows from the compositions \S\,69, (8) to (13) and from the transformation formulae for the $f$-functions, \S\,40.

For abbreviation we introduce the notation
\begin{equation}
 f(\omega)=u,
 \quad f_1(\omega)=u_1,
 \quad f_2(\omega)=u_2,
 \qquad
 f\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right)=v,
 \quad f_1\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right)=v_1,
 \quad f_2\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right)=v_2.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
Then the corresponding changes are obtained, if in the notation no account is taken of the transformation of the numbers $a,c,\partial$ into $a,c_1,\partial$ or $a_2,c_2,\partial_2$, as characterized by the cited formulae of \S\,69:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cccc}
substitution & $u$ & $u_1$ & $u_2$ \\
\hline
$\omega$ & $u$ & $u_1$ & $u_2$\\[0.2em]
$-1/\omega$ & $u$ & $u_2$ & $u_1$\\[0.2em]
$\omega+2$ & $u$ & $e^{\pi\ii/3}u_1$ & $e^{\pi\ii/3}u_2$\\[0.2em]
$(\omega-1)/(\omega+1)$ & $\lambda_1 u$ & $\lambda_2 u_2$ & $\lambda_3 u_1$
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
with analogous formulae for the $v$'s. The factors $\lambda_i$ are third roots of unity, and, when $n$ is not divisible by $3$, they are equal to $1$.

In order further to determine the effect of the substitution (2), which arises from the transformation of second degree
\[
 {\omega\over\omega+1},
\]
on the functions $u,v$, we must determine the transformations of first and $n$th order so that
\begin{equation}
 {c+\partial\omega\over a}
 ={\beta+\delta\omega'\over\alpha+\gamma\omega'},
 \qquad
 {c'+\partial'\omega'
  \over a'}
\quad\hbox{with}\quad
\omega'={\omega\over\omega+1},
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
which leads to the equations
\begin{equation}
 c'=\alpha c-\beta a+\gamma\partial+\delta c,
 \qquad
 a'=\alpha a+\beta c,
 \qquad
 \partial'=\gamma a+\delta\partial.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
From these equations $\partial'$ is first determined as the greatest common divisor of $a$ and $c+\partial$; from $n=a'\partial'$ one obtains $a'$, and then $c'$ can be chosen divisible by $16$, or by $48$ if necessary. After applying the transformation formulae of \S\,40 one obtains the corresponding interchanges
\begin{equation}
 \left(\omega,{\omega\over\omega+1}\right):
 \qquad u\leftrightarrow {2\over u},
 \qquad v\leftrightarrow {2\over v},
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
up to third roots of unity which again are $1$ when $n$ is not divisible by $3$.

We apply the interchanges (4), (5), (9) to the following functions:
\begin{equation}
 A=\left({u\over v}\right)^r+\left({v\over u}\right)^r,
 \qquad
 B=(uv)^s+\left({2\over n}\right)^{r+s}{2^s\over(uv)^s},
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
where $r,s$ are two integers satisfying
\begin{equation}
 (n-1)r\equiv0,
 \qquad
 (n+1)s\equiv0\pmod{12},
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
so that, if $n$ is divisible by $3$, both are divisible by $3$, and
\[
 \left({2\over n}\right)=(-1)^{(n^2-1)/8}.
\]
Then the following corresponding interchanges result:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cc}
substitution & $A$ & $B$\\
\hline
$-1/\omega$ & $A$ & $B$\\[0.2em]
$\omega+2$ & $(-1)^{(n-1)r/12}A$ & $(-1)^{(n+1)s/12}B$\\[0.2em]
$(\omega-1)/(\omega+1)$ & $\left({2\over n}\right)^r A$ & $\left({2\over n}\right)^s B$
\end{tabular}
\par\smallskip{\small (12)}
\end{center}
If now from $A,B$ one forms an integral rational function with numerical coefficients
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_{a,c,\partial}=\sum M_{h,k}A^hB^k,
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
where, if sign changes occur in (12), the exponents $h,k$ are to be arranged so that in all terms of (13) the same sign changes occur, then the system of functions $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$, or at least $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}^2$, satisfies condition 1 of the theorem just stated. To satisfy condition 2 as well, and thus to obtain a transformation equation, the coefficients $M_{h,k}$ must be determined so that all $\nu$ values of $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ remain finite for $q=0$.

This task is essentially simplified when $n$ has no quadratic divisor, and still more when $n$ is a prime number. If $n$ has no quadratic divisor, all values $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ can be derived from $\Phi_{a,0,\partial}$ by increasing $\omega$ by certain integers; hence if, as required, no negative powers occur in the development of $\Phi_{a,0,\partial}$ in ascending powers of $q$, the same is true of all $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$.

If, however, $n$ is a prime number, it is enough to prove that $\Phi_{1,0,n}$ contains no negative powers of $q$, since by interchanging $\omega$ with $\omega/n$ the same follows for $\Phi_{n,0,1}$.

The constant value which the function $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ obtains is determined from one term of the development. In carrying out these calculations one uses the developments \S\,24, (11),
\begin{align}
 A={}&q^{-(n-1)r/24}\prod_{h=1}^{\infty}
 \left({1+q^{n(2h-1)}\over1+q^{2h-1}}\right)^r
 +q^{(n-1)r/24}\prod_{h=1}^{\infty}
 \left({1+q^{2h-1}\over1+q^{n(2h-1)}}\right)^r,
\notag\\
 B={}&q^{-(n+1)s/24}\prod_{h=1}^{\infty}(1+q^{2h-1})^s(1+q^{n(2h-1)})^s
\notag\\
&+\left({2\over n}\right)^{r+s}q^{(n+1)s/24}2^s
\prod_{h=1}^{\infty}{1\over(1+q^{2h-1})^s(1+q^{n(2h-1)})^s}.
\tag{14}
\end{align}
The formulae are not always simplest when $r,s$ are taken as small as possible; rather it proves most expedient to add the condition
\begin{equation}
 {(n-1)r\over12}\equiv{(n+1)s\over12}\pmod2.
\tag{15}
\end{equation}

For the first seven odd primes one thus obtains the following determination of $A$ and $B$:
\begin{longtable}{>{$}c<{$} >{$}l<{$} >{$}l<{$}}
 n & A & B\\
\hline
3 & \left({u\over v}\right)^6+\left({v\over u}\right)^6
  & (uv)^3-{8\over(uv)^3}\\[0.5em]
5 & \left({u\over v}\right)^3+\left({v\over u}\right)^3
  & (uv)^2-{4\over(uv)^2}\\[0.5em]
7 & \left({u\over v}\right)^4+\left({v\over u}\right)^4
  & (uv)^3+{8\over(uv)^3}\\[0.5em]
11 & \left({u\over v}\right)^6+\left({v\over u}\right)^6
  & uv-{2\over uv}\\[0.5em]
13 & {u\over v}+{v\over u}
  & (uv)^6-{64\over(uv)^6}\\[0.5em]
17 & \left({u\over v}\right)^3+\left({v\over u}\right)^3
  & (uv)^4+{16\over(uv)^4}\\[0.5em]
19 & \left({u\over v}\right)^2+\left({v\over u}\right)^2
  & (uv)^3-{8\over(uv)^3}.
\end{longtable}

The developments in powers of $q$ for $A$ and $B$ follow from (14), as far as they are needed for the calculation, in this way:
\begin{align*}
 n=3:&\quad A=q^{-1/2}(1-6q+\cdots),\quad B=q^{-1/2}(1+\cdots);\\
 n=5:&\quad A=q^{-1/2}(1-3q+\cdots),\quad B=q^{-1/2}(1+\cdots);\\
 n=7:&\quad A=q^{-1}(1-4q+\cdots),\quad B=q^{-1}(1+3q+\cdots);\\
 n=11:&\quad A=q^{-5/2}(1-6q+\cdots),\quad B=q^{-1/2}(1+q+\cdots);\\
 n=13:&\quad A=q^{-1/2}(1-q+\cdots),\quad B=q^{-7/2}(1-6q+\cdots);\\
 n=17:&\quad A=q^{-2}(1-3q+6q^2-13q^3+25q^4-39q^5+76q^6+\cdots),\\
     &\quad B=q^{-3}(1+4q+4q^2+8q^3+14q^4+28q^5+49q^6+\cdots);\\
 n=19:&\quad A=q^{-3/2}(1-2q-3q^2+5q^3+11q^4-13q^5+24q^6-28q^7+\cdots),\\
     &\quad B=q^{-5/2}(1+3q+3q^2+4q^3+9q^4+4q^5+3q^6-27q^7+\cdots).
\end{align*}
Eliminating the negative powers gives the desired equations between $A$ and $B$:
\begin{align*}
 n=3:&\quad A+B=0,\\
 n=5:&\quad A-B+1=0,\\
 n=7:&\quad A^2-B+7=0,\\
 n=11:&\quad A-B^5-572B=0,\\
 n=13:&\quad B^2+A^7-24A-B=0,\\
 n=17:&\quad A^3-B^2+17AB-544A^2+53B+116A-440=0,\\
 n=19:&\quad A^5-B^3+19AB^2-95A^2B-109A^3+128B-128A=0.
\end{align*}
These equations were first set up by Schlaefli.
\footnote{Journal fuer Mathematik, vol. 72.}

From this system of equations a second and a third system are derived for the functions $f_1,f_2$, by replacing $\omega$ first by $\omega+1$ and then by $-1/\omega$. These two systems have the same form; one time one must put
\[
 u=f_1(\omega),\qquad v=f_1\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right),
\]
and the other time
\[
 u=f_2(\omega),\qquad v=f_2\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right).
\]
From the interchanges (4) one obtains, in this way:
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
\mathrm{II.}\quad n=3,&
 A_1=\left({u_1\over v_1}\right)^6-\left({v_1\over u_1}\right)^6,
 \quad B_1=(u_1v_1)^3+{8\over(u_1v_1)^3},\\[0.4em]
& A_1+B_1=0,\\[0.8em]
 n=5,&
 A_1=\left({u_1\over v_1}\right)^3-\left({v_1\over u_1}\right)^3,
 \quad B_1=(u_1v_1)^2+{4\over(u_1v_1)^2},\\[0.4em]
& A_1+B_1=0,\\[0.8em]
 n=7,&
 A_1=\left({u_1\over v_1}\right)^4+\left({v_1\over u_1}\right)^4,
 \quad B_1=(u_1v_1)^3+{8\over(u_1v_1)^3},\\[0.4em]
& A_1-B_1-7=0,\\[0.8em]
 n=11,&
 A_1=\left({u_1\over v_1}\right)^6-\left({v_1\over u_1}\right)^6,
 \quad B_1=u_1v_1+{2\over u_1v_1},\\[0.4em]
& A_1+B_1^5+B_1^3-2B_1=0,\\[0.8em]
 n=13,&
 A_1={u_1\over v_1}-{v_1\over u_1},
 \quad B_1=(u_1v_1)^6+{64\over(u_1v_1)^6},\\[0.4em]
& A_1^7-6A_1^5+A_1^3+20A_1+B_1=0,\\[0.8em]
 n=17,&
 A_1=\left({u_1\over v_1}\right)^3+\left({v_1\over u_1}\right)^3,
 \quad B_1=(u_1v_1)^4+{16\over(u_1v_1)^4},\\[0.4em]
& A_1^3-B_1^2-17A_1B_1-34A_1^2-34B_1+116A_1+440=0,\\[0.8em]
 n=19,&
 A_1=\left({u_1\over v_1}\right)^2-\left({v_1\over u_1}\right)^2,
 \quad B_1=(u_1v_1)^3+{8\over(u_1v_1)^3},\\[0.4em]
& A_1^5+B_1^3-19A_1B_1^2+95A_1^2B_1-109A_1^3+128B_1-128A_1=0.
\end{array}
\]

\section*{\S\,74. The form of the Schlaefli modular equations for a prime degree.}

The form which we found in the preceding paragraph for the relations existing between
\begin{equation}
 u=f(\omega),\qquad v=f\!\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right)
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
can, at least when the degree of transformation is a prime number $p$, easily be brought under a general law. Since in what follows much depends on this form, we shall enter here into somewhat more detail about it.

The determination of the numbers $r,s$ according to (11) and (15) of the preceding paragraph depends on the behavior of $p$ with respect to the modulus $24$; and since we may exclude the case $p=3$, we have the following cases:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 p\pmod{24} & r & s\\
\hline
 1  & 1  & 12\\
 5  & 3  & 2\\
 7  & 4  & 3\\
 11 & 6  & 1\\
 13 & 1  & 6\\
 17 & 3  & 4\\
 19 & 2  & 3\\
 23 & 12 & 1,
\end{array}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
so that $r+s$ is always odd, and $p+1$ is divisible by $2r$, $p-1$ by $2s$. Accordingly
\begin{equation}
 A=\left({u\over v}\right)^r+\left({v\over u}\right)^r,
 \qquad
 B=(uv)^s+\left({2\over p}\right){2^s\over(uv)^s}.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}

Now we know that the $p-1$ quantities $v$ are the roots of an irreducible transformation equation
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_p(v,u)=v^{p+1}+U_1v^p+\cdots+U_{p+1}=0,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
in which the coefficients $U_1,\ldots,U_{p+1}$ are rational functions of $u$.

We conclude at once that they are integral rational functions of $u$. For first, when $u=\infty$, all roots of (4) become infinite, as is seen by putting $\omega=i\infty$, hence letting $q=0$. Secondly, according to (9) of the preceding paragraph, by the interchange
\[
 \left(u,{\sqrt2\over u}\right)
\]
the totality of the roots $v$ goes over into
\[
 \left({2\over p}\right){\sqrt2\over v}.
\]
From this it follows that for $u=0$ all roots $v$ go over into zero, so that none of them becomes infinite. Hence one concludes not only that $U_1,U_2,\\ldots,U_{p+1}$ are integral functions of $u$, but also that each of them must contain the factor $u$.

We conclude next, exactly as for the invariant equation (\S\,69, 2., 3. and 6.), that
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_p(v,u)=\Phi_p(u,v),
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
and that
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_p(v,u)=(v^p-u)(v-u^p)
 +p\sum_{1,p}^{h,k} c_{h,k}u^hv^k,
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
where $c_{h,k}=c_{k,h}$ are integers.

We shall represent this function in the form
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_p(v,u)=v^{p+1}+u^{p+1}+\sum_{1,p}^{h,k} a_{h,k}u^hv^k,
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
where therefore $a_{h,k}=a_{k,h}$ are likewise integers, whose divisibility by $p$ is now no longer relevant, and in particular $a_{p,1}=-1$. If in the equation
\[
 \Phi_p[f(p\omega),f(\omega)]=0
\]
one replaces $\omega$ by $\omega+2$, then, because of irreducibility and on the basis of the relation
\[
 f(\omega+2)=e^{-\pi i/12}f(\omega),
\]
it follows that in (7) not all terms occur, but only those for which the exponents $h,k$ satisfy the congruence
\begin{equation}
 hp+k\equiv p+1\pmod{24}.
\tag{8}
\end{equation}

Now we know one more property of the functions $\Phi_p(v,u)$, which results from the interchange (9) of the preceding paragraph already used, where $\rho=1$ is to be put; if, for abbreviation, we put
\[
 \varepsilon=\left({2\over p}\right)=(-1)^{(p^2-1)/8},
\]
this property can be written
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_p(v,u)=\left({uv\over\sqrt2}\right)^{p+1}
 \Phi_p\!\left({\varepsilon\sqrt2\over v},{\sqrt2\over u}\right).
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
From this one obtains the relations
\begin{equation}
 \varepsilon^h2^{(h+k-p-1)/2}a_{p+1-h,p+1-k}=a_{h,k},
 \qquad a_{h,k}=a_{k,h}.
\tag{10}
\end{equation}

If, therefore, we combine in (7) the terms with equal coefficients $a_{h,k}$, we may restrict ourselves to the assumption that $h\ge k$, $h+k\ge p+1$; and, apart from the two terms $v^{p+1}$ and $u^{p+1}$, $\Phi_p$ appears as an aggregate of terms of the following two forms (taking into account also that, by (8), $\varepsilon^h=\varepsilon^k$):
\begin{align}
& v^hu^k+v^ku^h
 -\varepsilon^h2^{(h+k-p-1)/2}
 \bigl(u^{p+1-h}v^{p+1-k}+u^{p+1-k}v^{p+1-h}\bigr)
\notag\\
&\quad=(uv)^{(p+1)/2}
 \left[\left({v\over u}\right)^{(h-k)/2}
       +\left({u\over v}\right)^{(h-k)/2}\right]
 \left[(uv)^{(h+k-p-1)/2}
       +{\varepsilon^h2^{(h+k-p-1)/2}\over(uv)^{(h+k-p-1)/2}}\right],
\tag{11}
\end{align}
and
\begin{align}
& v^hu^h+\varepsilon^h2^{h-(p+1)/2}(uv)^{p+1-h}
\notag\\
&
=(uv)^{(p+1)/2}
 \left[(uv)^{h-(p+1)/2}
       +{\varepsilon^h2^{h-(p+1)/2}\over(uv)^{h-(p+1)/2}}\right].
\tag{12}
\end{align}
The coefficients of these terms in $\Phi_p$ are integers.

Now (8) gives
\[
 h-k\equiv(h-1)(p+1),
 \qquad
 h+k-p-1\equiv -h(p-1)
 \pmod{24},
\]
and hence $h-k$ is divisible by $2r$, and $h+k-p-1$ (where $h$ may also be equal to $k$) by $2s$ [\S\,74, (2)].

If, therefore, we put
\[
 {h-k\over2}=r\alpha,
 \qquad
 {h+k-p-1\over2}=s\beta,
\]
then $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are positive integers which lie between the bounds
\begin{equation}
 0\le\alpha<{p+1\over2r},
 \qquad
 0\le\beta\le {p-1\over2s},
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
and in addition the congruence following from (8), $-h(p-1)\equiv 2s\beta\pmod{24}$, shows that at least in the cases where $\varepsilon=-1$ one has $h\equiv\beta\pmod2$ [\S\,74, (2)], and hence always $\varepsilon^h=\varepsilon^\beta$.

Thus (11) becomes
\[
 (uv)^{(p+1)/2}
 \left[\left({v\over u}\right)^{r\alpha}+\left({u\over v}\right)^{r\alpha}\right]
 \left[(uv)^{s\beta}+{\varepsilon^\beta2^{s\beta}\over(uv)^{s\beta}}\right],
\]
and (12) becomes
\[
 (uv)^{(p+1)/2}
 \left[(uv)^{s\beta}+{\varepsilon^\beta2^{s\beta}\over(uv)^{s\beta}}\right].
\]

Now the well-known formulae hold, when $x,y$ are arbitrary quantities and
\[
 x+{\gamma\over x}=y
\]
is put:
\[
 x^2+{\gamma^2\over x^2}=y^2-2\gamma,
 \qquad
 x^3+{\gamma^3\over x^3}=y^3-3\gamma y,
 \ldots,
\]
\[
 \left(x^n+{\gamma^n\over x^n}\right)
 \left(x+{\gamma\over x}\right)
 =x^{n+1}+{\gamma^{n+1}\over x^{n+1}}
 +\gamma\left(x^{n-1}+{\gamma^{n-1}\over x^{n-1}}\right),
\]
from which one recognizes, by induction from $n$ to $n+1$, that
\[
 x^n+{\gamma^n\over x^n}
\]
can, for arbitrary $n$, be represented as an integral rational function of degree $n$ in $y$, which, if $\gamma$ is an integer, has integral coefficients and leading coefficient $1$.

The two quantities
\[
 \left({v\over u}\right)^{r\alpha}+\left({u\over v}\right)^{r\alpha}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 (uv)^{s\beta}+{\varepsilon^\beta2^{s\beta}\over(uv)^{s\beta}}
\]
can therefore be represented in this way as integral rational functions of $A$ and $B$ of degrees $\alpha$ and $\beta$, and we find, if we also separate off the term corresponding to $\beta=(p-1)/(2s)$, which has coefficient $-1$, and denote integral coefficients by $c_{\alpha,\beta}$:
\begin{equation}
 {\Phi_p(u,v)\over(uv)^{(p+1)/2}}
 =A^{(p+1)/(2r)}-B^{(p-1)/(2s)}
 +\sum c_{\alpha,\beta}A^\alpha B^\beta,
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
where $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are bound by the limits
\[
 0\le\alpha<{p+1\over 2r},
 \qquad
 0\le\beta<{p-1\over 2s}.
\]
This is the form which in the preceding paragraph we gave to the modular equations up to $p=19$.

It remains to mention that the consideration carried out in \S\,69, 4., 5. for the invariant equation in the case of composite transformation degree applies unchanged also to the Schlaefli modular equations. Hence we may conclude that all these equations have rational numerical coefficients.

\clearpage

\section*{\S~75. The irrational forms of the modular equations.}

By applying the same procedure, much simpler forms can be given to the transformation equations. The equation forms with which we shall now be concerned contain the three functions $f,f_1,f_2$ simultaneously; but since, by \S~34, (11), (12), two of these functions can be expressed by the third, two of them can be eliminated, and one can thus arrive at the equations of the preceding paragraph. If one substitutes for $f_1,f_2$ their expressions through $f$, or for the three functions $f,f_1,f_2$ their expressions through $k^2$, radical signs occur; from this the name of these equations is explained.

We put, as above,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u&=f(\omega),& v&=f\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right),\\
u_1&=f_1(\omega),& v_1&=\left({2\over a}\right)f_1\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right),\\
u_2&=f_2(\omega),& v_2&=\left({2\over\partial}\right)f_2\left({c+\partial\omega\over a}\right),\\
u u_1u_2&=\sqrt2,& vv_1v_2&=\left({2\over n}\right)\sqrt2,
\end{aligned}
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
and obtain the following associated interchanges:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
\omega&uv&u_1v_1&u_2v_2\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho uv&\varrho u_2v_2&\varrho u_1v_1\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{-(n+1)\pi i/24}\sigma u_1v_1&e^{-(n+1)\pi i/24}\sigma uv&e^{(n+1)\pi i/12}\sigma u_2v_2,
\end{array}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $\varrho,\sigma$ are the third roots of unity defined above in \S~73, (4).

We distinguish three different cases according to the behavior of $n$ modulo $8$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{1.} $n+1\equiv0\pmod8$.

We put
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 2A&=uv+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}(u_1v_1+u_2v_2),\\
 B&=uvu_1v_1+uvu_2v_2+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}u_1v_1u_2v_2\\
 &= {2\over u_1v_1}+{2\over u_2v_2}+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}{2\over uv},
\end{aligned}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
so that the associated interchanges become
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\omega&A&B\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho A&\varrho^2B\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{\pi i(n+1)/12}\sigma A&e^{-\pi i(n+1)/12}\sigma^2B.
\end{array}
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
A product of the form $A^hB^k$ therefore takes, under the two interchanges
\[
 \left(\omega,-{1\over\omega}\right),\qquad (\omega,\omega+1),
\]
the factors
\[
 \varrho^{h-k},\qquad e^{(h-k)(n+1)\pi i/12}\sigma^{h-k},
\]
which are equal to $1$ when $n+1$ is divisible by $3$. We therefore now form, with numerical coefficients $M_{h,k}$, functions of the form
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_{a,c,\partial}=\sum M_{h,k}A^hB^k,
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
where, if $n\equiv0$ or $1\pmod3$, $h$ and $k$ may assume only integral values whose differences $h-k$ leave the same residue on division by $3$, while if $n\equiv-1\pmod3$ they are not subject to this restriction. Such a function itself, or at least its third power, therefore satisfies a transformation equation of the first stage. It also remains finite for all finite values of $j(\omega)$, and consequently is an integral algebraic function of $j(\omega)$. [The transformation of the second order $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}$ need not be brought in here, since the totality of the $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ remains unchanged already under the substitution $(\omega,\omega+1)$, not only under $(\omega,\omega+2)$. Compare the remark at the beginning of \S~73.]

If, therefore, we determine the constants in $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ so that no negative powers of $q$ occur in the expansions of these functions, then all the $\Phi_{a,c,\partial}$ must be equal to one and the same constant.

To reach this goal it is sufficient here also, if $n$ has no square divisor, that $\Phi_{1,0,n}$, and if $n$ is a prime number, that $\Phi_{1,0,n}$, remain finite for $q=0$.

In these calculations we use the expansions
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
uv&=q^{-(n+1)/24}\prod(1+q^{n(2h+1)})(1+q^{2h+1}),\\
u_1v_1&=q^{-(n+1)/24}\prod(1-q^{n(2h+1)})(1-q^{2h+1}),\\
u_2v_2&=2q^{(n+1)/12}\prod(1+q^{2hn})(1+q^{2h}),
\end{aligned}
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
from which, by expansion in powers of $q$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
uv&=q^{-(n+1)/24}(1+q+q^3+q^4+q^5+q^6-q^7+2q^8\cdots),\\
u_1v_1&=q^{-(n+1)/24}(1-q-q^3+q^4-q^5+q^6-q^7+2q^8\cdots),\\
u_2v_2&=2q^{(n+1)/12}(1+q^2+q^4+2q^6+2q^8+\cdots).
\end{aligned}
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
The latter formulae are correct for $n>7$; for $n=3,5,7$ the terms from $q^3,q^5,q^7$ on are to be modified. For $n=7$, $n=23$ these expressions show that $A$ itself remains finite for $q=0$, whence for these cases the equations follow:
\begin{equation}
 n=7,\quad A=0,\qquad n=23,\quad A=1.
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
By a simple calculation one further finds:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rl}
n=31,& (A^2-B)^2-A=0,\\
n=47,& A^2-A-B=2,\\
n=71,& A^3-4A^2+2A-B=1.
\end{array}
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
The calculation is not quite so simple for composite $n$. For example, for $n=15$ one must take account of the condition of finiteness for $q=0$ not only for $\Phi_{1,0,15}$, but also for $\Phi_{3,0,5}$. These two, however, are sufficient. One obtains:
\begin{equation}
 n=15,
 \qquad A^3-AB+1=0.
\tag{10}
\end{equation}

\noindent\textbf{2.} If $n\equiv3\pmod8$, the same conclusions are to be drawn if we put
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
4A&=u^2v^2-u_1^2v_1^2-u_2^2v_2^2,\\
B&=u^2v^2u_1^2v_1^2+u^2v^2u_2^2v_2^2-u_1^2v_1^2u_2^2v_2^2,
\end{aligned}
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
for which one obtains from (2) the associated interchanges
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\omega&A&B\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho^2A&\varrho B\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{(n+1)\pi i/6}\sigma^2A&e^{-(n+1)\pi i/6}\sigma B,
\end{array}
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
and from this, in the same way, one derives the equations
\begin{equation}
 n=3,\quad A=0,
 \qquad n=11,\quad A=1,
 \qquad n=19,\quad A^5-7A^2-B=0.
\tag{13}
\end{equation}

\noindent\textbf{3.} If $n\equiv1\pmod4$, the same procedure can be followed with the functions
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
8A&=u^4v^4-u_1^4v_1^4-u_2^4v_2^4,\\
B&=u^4v^4u_1^4v_1^4+u^4v^4u_2^4v_2^4-u_1^4v_1^4u_2^4v_2^4,
\end{aligned}
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
for which one obtains the associated interchanges
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\omega&A&B\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho A&\varrho^2B\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{(n+1)\pi i/3}\sigma A&e^{-(n+1)\pi i/3}\sigma^2B.
\end{array}
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
Only the first case, $n=5$, leads here to a simple result:
\begin{equation}
 n=5,
 \qquad A=1.
\tag{16}
\end{equation}

For $n=5$ an even simpler form of the transformation equation can be obtained. If we apply the substitutions $(\omega,-1/\omega)$ and $(\omega,\omega+1)$ to the three functions
\begin{equation}
 w={u_1^2v_2^2+u_2^2v_1^2\over uv},\qquad
 w_1={u_2^2v^2-v_2^2u^2\over u_1v_1},\qquad
 w_2={u_1^2v^2-v_1^2u^2\over u_2v_2},
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
then, under the assumption $n=5\pmod8$, the associated interchanges become
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
\omega&w&w_1&w_2\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&w&w_2&w_1\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{-\pi i(n-5)/24}w_1&e^{-\pi i(n-5)/24}w&e^{\pi i(n-5)/12}w_2.
\end{array}
\]
Thus, if one sets
\[
 A=w^2+w_1^2+w_2^2,
\]
\[
 B=w^2w_1^2+w^2w_2^2+w_1^2w_2^2,
\]
\[
 C=ww_1w_2,
\]
one obtains
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
\omega&A&B&C\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&A&B&C\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{-\pi i(n-5)/12}A&e^{-\pi i(n-5)/6}B&C,
\end{array}
\]
so that two of these functions can be used just as $A$ and $B$ above. But for $n=5$ it appears that no negative powers occur in the expansions of $w,w_1,w_2$ in powers of $q$, and therefore that $A,B,C$, and consequently also $w,w_1,w_2$ themselves, are constant.

Thus the modular equation for $n=5$ can be put in each of the three forms
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 u_1^2v_2^2+u_2^2v_1^2&=2uv,\\
 u_2^2v^2-v_2^2u^2&=2u_1v_1,\\
 u_1^2v^2-v_1^2u^2&=2u_2v_2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
These equations can also be derived from the equation given by Jacobi (Fund. art. 30, collected works, vol. 1, p. 123).

We close these considerations by deriving, in the simplest cases, the Jacobian modular equations from these irrational forms.

We put [cf. \S~54, (3)]
\begin{equation}
 {u_2\over u}=x=\sqrt[4]{k'},\qquad {u_1\over u}=x'=\sqrt[4]{k''},
 \qquad
 {v_2\over v}=y=\sqrt[4]{\lambda'},\qquad {v_1\over v}=y'=\sqrt[4]{\lambda''},
\tag{19a}
\end{equation}
and eliminate the quantities $u$ and $v$ by means of the relations
\begin{equation}
 x^8+x'^8=1,
 \qquad xx'={\sqrt2\over u^3},
 \qquad
 y^8+y'^8=1,
 \qquad yy'={\sqrt2\over v^3}.
\tag{19}
\end{equation}

For $n=3$ one obtains from (13)
\begin{equation}
 x^2y^2+x'^2y'^2=1,
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
a form of the modular equation which is due to Legendre. From it, by inserting for $x',y'$ the values from (19),
\[
 x^8+y^8=4x^2y^2-6x^4y^4+4x^6y^6,
\]
or
\[
 (x^4-y^4)^2=4(xy-x^3y^3)^2,
\]
and then extracting the root and determining the sign by $q=0$, one obtains
\begin{equation}
 x^4-y^4=2xy-2x^3y^3,
 \qquad (n=3),
\tag{21}
\end{equation}
which, apart from the sign of $\sqrt[4]{\lambda}$ not explained more fully there, agrees with \S~10, (12).

For $n=5$ the second equation (18) gives
\[
 (x^2-y^2)^3=4xyx'^4y'^4,
\]
and hence, by squaring,
\[
 (x^2-y^2)^6=16x^2y^2(1-x^8-y^8+x^8y^8),
\]
which is easily brought into the form
\[
 (x^2-y^2)^2[(x^2-y^2)^2+8x^2y^2]^2=16x^2y^2(1-x^4y^4)^2.
\]
Extracting the root gives, as above,
\begin{equation}
 x^6-y^6-4xy(1-x^4y^4)+5x^2y^2(x^2-y^2)=0\qquad(n=5).
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
For $n=7$, without extracting roots, one obtains from (8):
\begin{equation}
 xy+x'y'=1,
\tag{23}
\end{equation}
and from this
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 x^8+y^8&-8xy(1+x^6y^6)+28x^2y^2(1+x^4y^4)\\
&-56x^3y^3(1+x^2y^2)+70x^4y^4=0\qquad(n=7).
\end{aligned}
\tag{24}
\end{equation}

\section*{\S~76. Composite transformation degrees.}

If the transformation degree is a composite number, then one can set up still simpler transformation equations than those obtained by the path of the preceding paragraph.

We carry out these considerations here only in the simplest cases.

Let the odd transformation degree $n$ be decomposed into two factors
\begin{equation}
 n=n'n'',
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
which are relatively prime to one another.

To each transformation of degree $n$ of the form
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}a&0\\ c&\partial\end{pmatrix}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
there can then be assigned one and only one transformation of the degrees $n',n''$:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}a'&0\\ c'&\partial'\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \begin{pmatrix}a''&0\\ c''&\partial''\end{pmatrix},
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
determined by the following conditions:
\begin{equation}
 a=a'a'',\qquad \partial=\partial'\partial'',\qquad
 \partial''c'\equiv c\pmod {a'},\qquad
 \partial'c''\equiv c\pmod {a''},
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and conversely, from every pair of transformations of the form (3), according to (4), one and only one transformation (2) follows. For by (4) $\partial',\partial''$ are first determined as the greatest common divisors of $\partial$ with $n'$ and $n''$; then $c'$ is determined modulo $a'$, and $c''$ modulo $a''$, from the two last congruences (4).

It is now chiefly important to show that the congruences (4) remain preserved when the transformations (2) and (3), according to \S~69, (8) to (12), are transformed by the two linear transformations
\[
 (\omega,\omega+1),\qquad \left(\omega,-{1\over\omega}\right).
\]

According to the formulae just mentioned we set:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\ c&\partial\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ 1&1\end{pmatrix}
&=
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \lambda&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\ c_1&\partial\end{pmatrix},\\
\begin{pmatrix}a'&0\\ c'&\partial'\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ 1&1\end{pmatrix}
&=
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \lambda'&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}a'&0\\ c'_1&\partial'\end{pmatrix},\\
\begin{pmatrix}a''&0\\ c''&\partial''\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ 1&1\end{pmatrix}
&=
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \lambda''&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}a''&0\\ c''_1&\partial''\end{pmatrix},
\end{aligned}
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\ c&\partial\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\ -1&0\end{pmatrix}
&=
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}a_2&0\\ c_2&\partial_2\end{pmatrix},\\
\begin{pmatrix}a'&0\\ c'&\partial'\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\ -1&0\end{pmatrix}
&=
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\ \gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}a'_2&0\\ c'_2&\partial'_2\end{pmatrix},\\
\begin{pmatrix}a''&0\\ c''&\partial''\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\ -1&0\end{pmatrix}
&=
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha''&\beta''\\ \gamma''&\delta''\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}a''_2&0\\ c''_2&\partial''_2\end{pmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
First, by \S~69, (9), one has
\[
 c_1\equiv c+\partial\pmod a,
 \qquad c'_1\equiv c'+\partial'\pmod {a'},
 \qquad c''_1\equiv c''+\partial''\pmod {a''},
\]
whence, by (4),
\[
 \partial''c'_1\equiv \partial''c'+\partial\equiv c_1\pmod {a'},
\]
\[
 \partial'c''_1\equiv \partial'c''+\partial\equiv c_1\pmod {a''},
\]
in agreement with the congruences (4).

For the composition (6), according to \S~69, (12), $\partial_2,\partial'_2,\partial''_2$ are the greatest common divisors of $a,c$; of $a',c'$; and of $a'',c''$; since however $\partial''$ is relatively prime to $a'$ and $\partial''c'\equiv c\pmod {a'}$, $\partial'_2$ is also the greatest common divisor of $a'$ and $c$, and for the same reasons $\partial''_2$ is the greatest common divisor of $a''$ and $c$. It follows that $\partial'_2,\partial''_2$ are relatively prime:
\[
 \partial_2=\partial'_2\partial''_2,
 \qquad a_2=a'_2a''_2.
\]
Further, by \S~69, (11), (12), (13),
\[
 \partial_2\alpha=-c\gamma-a,
 \qquad c_2=-\partial\alpha,
\]
\[
 \partial'_2\alpha'=\alpha'c'-\partial' a',
 \qquad c'_2=-\partial'\alpha',
\]
\[
 \partial''_2\alpha''=\alpha''c''-\partial''a'',
 \qquad c''_2=-\partial''\alpha'',
\]
and hence, by (4),
\[
 \partial'_2c''_2-c_2\partial''_2\equiv \alpha\alpha'(\partial c'-c\partial')\equiv0\pmod {a'},
\]
therefore also
\[
 \partial'_2c''_2-c_2\partial''_2\equiv0\pmod {a'_2},
\]
in agreement with (4); and in the same way
\[
 \partial''_2c'_2-c_2\partial'_2\equiv0\pmod {a''_2}.
\]
Thus the proof is completed that the association of the transformations expressed by (4),
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}a&0\\ c&\partial\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}a'&0\\ c'&\partial'\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}a''&0\\ c''&\partial''\end{pmatrix},
\]
is not disturbed by applying any linear transformation to $\omega$. Since here we suppose $n$ odd, we can always assume $c,c',c''$ divisible by $16$; and if $n$, and consequently also $n',n''$, are not divisible by $3$, then $c,c',c''$ can also be assumed divisible by $3$. But if $n$ is divisible by $3$, then one of the two factors $n',n''$, say $n''$, will be divisible by $3$, the other $n'$ not. Then $c$ may still be assumed divisible by $3$, but not $c'$ and $c''$. In this case the dependence of $c''$ on $c$ is to be determined more precisely by the congruence
\begin{equation}
 \partial'c''\equiv c\pmod {3a''}.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
A solution of this congruence can always be derived from a solution of the congruence (4), $\partial'c''\equiv c\pmod {a''}$, by adding to $c''$ a multiple of $a''$.

The congruence (7) then has, by \S~69, (9) to (13), the consequence
\begin{equation}
 \lambda\equiv \lambda'\lambda''\pmod 3,
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \alpha\equiv \alpha''\partial'_2,
 \quad \beta\equiv \beta''a'_2,
 \quad \gamma\equiv \gamma''a'_2,
 \quad \delta\equiv \delta''\partial'_2\pmod 3.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

Let now $v',v'_1,v'_2;v'',v''_1,v''_2$ have the same meaning for the numbers $n',n''$ that $v,v_1,v_2$ had in \S~73, (3) for the number $n$, namely
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
v'&=f\left({c'+\partial'\omega\over a'}\right),
&v''&=f\left({c''+\partial''\omega\over a''}\right),\\
v'_1&=\left({2\over a'}\right)f_1\left({c'+\partial'\omega\over a'}\right),
&v''_1&=\left({2\over a''}\right)f_1\left({c''+\partial''\omega\over a''}\right),\\
v'_2&=\left({2\over\partial'}\right)f_2\left({c'+\partial'\omega\over a'}\right),
&v''_2&=\left({2\over\partial''}\right)f_2\left({c''+\partial''\omega\over a''}\right).
\end{aligned}
\tag{10}
\end{equation}

We apply the interchange table (4), \S~73, to these functions. Since $n'$ is in all circumstances not divisible by $3$, the cubic roots of unity $\varrho',\sigma'$ are to be set equal to $1$, while by the congruences (8), (9)
\begin{equation}
 \varrho''=\varrho^{n'},\qquad \sigma''=\sigma^{n'}
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
results. Thus we obtain the following associated interchanges:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
\omega&v'&v'_1&v'_2\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&v'&v'_2&v'_1\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&e^{-n'\pi i/24}v'_1&e^{-n'\pi i/24}v'&e^{n'\pi i/12}v'_2\\[0.3em]\hline
\omega&v''&v''_1&v''_2\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho^{n'}v''&\varrho^{n'}v''_2&\varrho^{n'}v''_1\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&\sigma^{n'}e^{-n''\pi i/24}v''_1&\sigma^{n'}e^{-n''\pi i/24}v''&\sigma^{n'}e^{n''\pi i/12}v''_2,
\end{array}
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
which hold together with the interchanges (4), \S~73.

We now distinguish two cases.

\noindent\textbf{1.} If
\begin{equation}
 (n'+1)(n''+1)=8\mu\equiv0\pmod 8,
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
then we put
\begin{equation}
 U=uv'v'',
 \qquad U_1=u_1v'_1v''_1,
 \qquad U_2=u_2v'_2v''_2,
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
2A&=U+(-1)^\mu(U_1+U_2),\\
B&=UU_1+UU_2+(-1)^\mu U_1U_2\\
&={4\over U_1}+{4\over U_2}+(-1)^\mu{4\over U}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
From (12) and \S~73, (4), we then obtain the following associated interchanges:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\omega&A&B\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho^{n'+1}A&\varrho^{-(n'+1)}B\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&\sigma^{n'+1}e^{2\mu\pi i/3}A&\sigma^{-(n'+1)}e^{-2\mu\pi i/3}B.
\end{array}
\tag{16}
\end{equation}
We apply our principle for deriving modular equations to these functions, and add the following remarks.

The third roots of unity occurring in (16) are equal to $1$ if either $n$ is not divisible by $3$ and $\mu$ is divisible by $3$, or $n''$ is divisible by $3$ and $n'$ leaves the residue $2$. In these cases every rational function of $A$ and $B$ is a root of a transformation equation. In the other cases this property belongs to the cube of such a rational function of $A$ and $B$ in which the differences of the exponents of all terms are congruent to one another modulo $3$.

If these rational functions are whole functions, and if in addition all their values are finite for $q=0$, then they must be equal to a constant, and thereby we obtain transformation equations.

If $n',n''$ are prime numbers, then it is sufficient here also, as in \S~72, that the negative powers of $q$ disappear in the expansion of such a function in increasing powers of $q$ in the one principal case, namely the case in which
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}a&0\\ c&\partial\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}a'&0\\ c'&\partial'\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}a''&0\\ c''&\partial''\end{pmatrix}
\]
are equal to
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ 0&n\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ 0&n'\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ 0&n''\end{pmatrix},
\]
that is,
\[
 U=f(\omega)f(n'\omega)f(n''\omega)f(n'n''\omega);
\]
for from the expansion for this one case the expansions for the remaining cases can be derived by replacing $\omega$ by
\begin{equation}
 {\omega\over n},\quad {\omega\over n'},\quad {\omega\over n''},
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
and then also increasing $\omega$ by whole numbers, whereby no negative powers of $q$ can be newly introduced.

For carrying out the calculation one uses the expansions
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
U&=q^{-(n'+1)(n''+1)/24}\prod(1+q^{2h-1})(1+q^{(2h-1)n'})(1+q^{(2h-1)n''})(1+q^{(2h-1)n}),\\
U_1&=q^{-(n'+1)(n''+1)/24}\prod(1-q^{2h-1})(1-q^{(2h-1)n'})(1-q^{(2h-1)n''})(1-q^{(2h-1)n}),\\
U_2&=4q^{(n'+1)(n''+1)/12}\prod(1+q^{2h})(1+q^{2hn'})(1+q^{2hn''})(1+q^{2hn}),
\end{aligned}
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
which, in the individual cases, give the power expansions of $A,B$, from which the negative powers of $q$ are to be eliminated. In this way one calculates very simply the following equations:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rl}
n=15,& A=1,\\
n=21,& (A^2-B)^2-A=0,\\
n=33,& A^2-B-A=4,\\
n=35,& A^2-B-A=2,\\
n=55,& A^3-B-4A^2-A+4=0.
\end{array}
\tag{19}
\end{equation}

\noindent\textbf{2.} If
\[
 (n'-1)(n''-1)=8\mu\equiv0\pmod8,
\]
then we put
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A&={uv\over v'v''}+(-1)^\mu\left({u_1v_1\over v'_1v''_1}+{u_2v_2\over v'_2v''_2}\right),\\
B&={v'v''\over uv}+(-1)^\mu\left({v'_1v''_1\over u_1v_1}+{v'_2v''_2\over u_2v_2}\right),
\end{aligned}
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
and obtain from (12) the associated interchanges
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\omega&A&B\\[0.2em]
-1/\omega&\varrho^{1-n'}A&\varrho^{n'-1}B\\[0.2em]
\omega+1&\sigma^{1-n'}e^{2\mu\pi i/3}A&\sigma^{n'-1}e^{-2\mu\pi i/3}B.
\end{array}
\tag{21}
\end{equation}
Therefore we can apply the same procedure as above, if we add the restriction that only symmetric functions of $A$ and $B$, that is, rational functions of $AB$ and $A+B$, are used; for only under this assumption do all remaining values of such a function follow from one of its values by the interchanges (17). One easily calculates the following examples:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rl}
n=15,& AB+1=0,\\
n=35,& 2(A+B)-AB=5,\\
n=39,& 2(A+B)-AB=3.
\end{array}
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
The calculation presents no insurmountable difficulties even in still more complicated cases. Thus, in the paper in \emph{Acta mathematica}, vol. II, p. 359, for the case $n=105$, I communicated the following formula:
\[
 A=\sum {f(3\omega)f(5\omega)f(7\omega)f(105\omega)\over f(\omega)f(15\omega)f(21\omega)f(35\omega)},
\]
\[
 B=\sum {f(\omega)f(15\omega)f(21\omega)f(35\omega)\over f(3\omega)f(5\omega)f(7\omega)f(105\omega)},
\]
\[
\begin{aligned}
 A^2+B^2&-4(A+B)^3+10AB(A+B)\\
&+4(A+B)^2+10AB+14(A+B)+5=0,
\end{aligned}
\]
where the sums $\sum$ in $A$ and $B$ refer to the three functions $f,f_1,f_2$.


\clearpage

\section*{\S~77. Geometric interpretation of the irrational modular equations as modular correspondences.}

We shall make the content of the irrational forms of the modular equations more vivid by a geometric interpretation. First consider the case $n\equiv -1\pmod 8$ and put
\begin{equation}
 x=f(\omega),\qquad y=f_1(\omega),\qquad z=f_2(\omega).
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
Then, if $x,y,z$ are regarded as Cartesian coordinates of a point $P$, a space curve is represented, which we may also express by the two equations
\begin{equation}
 x^8-y^8-z^8=0,
 \qquad
 xyz=\sqrt{2},
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
and which we shall call the fundamental curve. This curve is therefore of order $24$. It can be mapped onto a plane curve of order $3$ if one puts $x^8=X$, $y^8=Y$:
\begin{equation}
 XY(X-Y)=16.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
Putting [\S~75, (1)]
\begin{equation}
 \xi=f\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),\qquad
 \eta=\left({2\over\delta}\right)f_1\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),\qquad
 \zeta=\left({2\over a}\right)f_2\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
the quantities $\xi,\eta,\zeta$ also satisfy the equations (2), since
\[
 \left({2\over a}\right)\left({2\over\delta}\right)=\left({2\over n}\right)=\pm1,
\]
and the point $\Pi$ whose coordinates are $\xi,\eta,\zeta$ therefore also lies on the fundamental curve.

An equation
\[
 \Phi(x,y,z,\xi,\eta,\zeta)=0
\]
means, when the point $P$ is held fixed, a surface on which $\Pi$ is to lie, and the intersection of this surface with the fundamental curve gives a certain number of points $\Pi$ corresponding to the point $P$. Likewise a certain number of points $P$ correspond to a fixed point $\Pi$, and this assignment is called a correspondence on the fundamental curve. If the point $P$ coincides with one of the points $\Pi$ corresponding to it, we obtain a double point or coincidence of the correspondence.

If equation (5) is not changed when $P$ and $\Pi$ are interchanged, the correspondence is reciprocal. Such correspondences are given by the equations (8), (9), (10), \S~75.

Then by \S~75, (3), one has to put
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
2A&=x\xi+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}(y\eta+z\zeta),\\
B&=x\xi y\eta+x\xi z\zeta+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}y\eta z\zeta.
\end{aligned}
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
$A$ is of the first degree, and $B$ of the second degree, with respect to the coordinates of each of the two points $P$ and $\Pi$.

We shall determine the degree of these correspondences, that is, the number of points $\Pi$ corresponding to one point $P$. For this purpose we must multiply the degree of the equation $\Phi=0$ with respect to $\xi,\eta,\zeta$ by the degree of the fundamental curve, namely by $24$, and one obtains:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{for } n=7,&m=24,\\
 n=23,&m=24,\\
 n=31,&m=96,\\
 n=47,&m=48,\\
 n=71,&m=72,\\
 n=15,&m=72.
\end{array}
\]
In the cases $23,47,71$, therefore, the degree of the correspondence is equal to the degree of the corresponding modular equation, that is, equal to the number of the transformations in question; in the cases $n=7,31,15$ the degree is three times the degree of the modular equations.

What is the significance of these excess points? They are explained by the fact that in these cases, in which $n\equiv0,1\pmod 3$, in the function $\Phi_{a,c,\delta}$ [\S~75, (5)], which according to the above equation (5) is equal to a constant, the exponents $h-2k$ in all terms have the same residue modulo $3$, and hence the equation $\Phi=0$, and likewise the equations (2), remain satisfied when $\xi,\eta,\zeta$ are multiplied by any third root of unity. Thus every three points of the correspondence give the same transformation.

We might also, when $x,y,z$ are determined by (1), have put instead of (4)
\begin{equation}
\xi=f\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),\qquad
\eta=\left({2\over\delta}\right)f_2\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),\qquad
\zeta=\left({2\over a}\right)f_1\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
and consequently for $A,B$:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
2A&=x\xi+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}(y\zeta+z\eta),\\
B&=x\xi y\zeta+x\xi z\eta+(-1)^{(n+1)/8}y\eta z\zeta.
\end{aligned}
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
The fundamental curve and the degree of the correspondence would then have remained the same, but the coincidences would have acquired another, and indeed a simpler, meaning. In both cases the coincidences belong to the singular values of the modular functions, in which $\omega$ is an imaginary quadratic irrationality, as we shall see more precisely later.

In the case $n\equiv3\pmod 8$ we put
\[
 x=f^2(\omega),\qquad y=f_1^2(\omega),\qquad z=f_2^2(\omega),
\]
\begin{equation}
 \xi=f^2\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),\qquad
 \eta=f_1^2\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),\qquad
 \rho=f_2^2\left({c+\delta\omega\over a}\right),
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
and obtain a fundamental curve
\begin{equation}
 x^4-y^4-z^4=0,
 \qquad
 xyz=2,
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
which is only of order $12$. We then have, further, by \S~75,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
4A&=x\xi-y\eta-z\rho,\\
B&=x\xi y\eta+x\xi z\rho-y\eta z\rho,
\end{aligned}
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
and for the degree $m$ of the correspondence one obtains, according to \S~75 (13),
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
 n=3,&m=12,\\
 n=11,&m=12,\\
 n=19,&m=60.
\end{array}
\]
Thus again, as in the preceding case, when $n\equiv0,1\pmod 3$, a number three times too large is obtained.

Finally, if $n\equiv1\pmod4$, one puts
\[
 x=f^4(\omega),\qquad y=f_1^4(\omega),\qquad z=f_2^4(\omega)
\]
and obtains a fundamental curve of the sixth degree.

For $n=5$ the correct number $m=6$ results.

\section*{Eighth Section. The group of the transformation equations and the equation of the fifth degree.}

\section*{\S~78. The Galois group of the transformation equations for a prime degree.}

A more penetrating algebraic study of the transformation equations requires knowledge of their Galois group. Since we have derived the transformation equations from the division equations, whose group is known to us (\S~63), we can likewise form the group of the transformation equations.

The transformation equations arose (\S~65) because the roots of the division equations could be divided into series which were not torn apart by the interchanges of the group of the division equation, but only interchanged among themselves.

To each of these series there corresponds a definite root of a transformation equation, and hence the group of the latter consists of the totality of the interchanges produced by the group of the division equation among the series.

If the transformation degree $n$ is an odd prime number $p$, this group admits a very elegant representation, suitable for further investigations, and we shall now retain this assumption.

In \S~65, (3), the necessary and sufficient condition was already found that two roots of the division equation
\begin{equation}
 x_{\mu,\mu'},\qquad x_{\nu,\nu'}
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
belong to the same series $R$, namely the congruence
\begin{equation}
 \mu\nu'-\nu\mu'\equiv0\pmod p.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
If now, as is customary in number theory (Gauss, \emph{Disquisitiones arithmeticae}, art. 31), by the symbol
\[
 {a\over b}\pmod p
\]
we understand an integer [or residue class (mod $p$)] which, when multiplied by $b$, leaves the residue $a$ upon division by $p$, then, provided that $\mu'\nu'$ are not divisible by $p$, the congruence (2) defining a series can also be written
\begin{equation}
 {\mu\over \mu'}\equiv {\nu\over \nu'}\pmod p,
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
and we are therefore naturally led to designate the series $R$ by the value of the ratio
\begin{equation}
 {\mu\over \mu'}\equiv z\pmod p,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
which may be congruent to any of the numbers $0,1,\ldots,p-1$, and which is invariant for an entire series. At first the one series remains undesignated in which $\mu'$, and therefore all $\nu'$, are divisible by $p$; but this series also fits very well into the general notation if, in case $\mu'\equiv0\pmod p$, we put
\begin{equation}
 {\mu\over \mu'}\equiv\infty\pmod p,
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
so that we still obtain a $(p+1)$st series $R_\infty$. The totality of the series, whose number is $p+1$, is therefore to be denoted by
\begin{equation}
 R_\infty,R_0,R_1,\ldots,R_{p-1}.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
Correspondingly, the associated roots of a transformation equation are to be denoted by
\begin{equation}
 v_\infty,v_0,v_1,\ldots,v_{p-1}.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}

If, for example, we take the invariant equation (\S~69) as basis, then, according to the determinations of \S~68, (10), about the numbers $a,c,\delta$, one has to put
\begin{equation}
 v_\infty=j(p\omega),
 \qquad
 v_z=j\left({z+\omega\over p}\right),
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
and similarly if any other transformation equation is chosen.

With this notation we are able to derive at once the group of the transformation equation from that of the division equation.

We first take as rationality domain the totality of the rational functions of $x^2$ with rational numerical coefficients.

According to \S~63, 3., in this rationality domain the group of the division equation consists of all substitutions by which $\mu,\mu'$ are transformed into
\[
 \delta\mu-b\mu',
 \qquad
 -c\mu+a\mu',
\]
where $a,b,c,\delta$ are arbitrary integers taken modulo $p$, whose determinant
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=a\delta-bc
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
is not divisible by $p$, and the number of all these substitutions is
\begin{equation}
 p(p-1)(p^2-1) \qquad [\S~63,(27)].
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
From this, however, by the notation (4), (5), the group of the transformation equation is obtained as consisting of all interchanges expressed by the symbol
\begin{equation}
 \left(z,{\delta z-b\over -cz+a}\right)\pmod p.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
We denote a substitution $(z,z')$, if
\begin{equation}
 z\equiv {c+\delta z'\over a+bz'}\pmod p,
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
similarly as before by
\begin{equation}
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\ c&\delta\end{pmatrix},
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
and obtain for the composition of two such substitutions, if
\[
 z'\equiv {c'+\delta' z''\over a'+b'z''}\pmod p,
\]
the rule
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
(z,z')(z',z'')&=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\ c&\delta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a'&b'\\ c'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}\\
&\equiv\begin{pmatrix}aa'+bc'&ab'+b\delta'\\ ca'+\delta c'&cb'+\delta\delta'\end{pmatrix}\pmod p,
\end{aligned}
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
in agreement with the rule for the composition of two transformations in \S~28. The substitution (11) is therefore to be denoted by
\[
\begin{pmatrix}\delta&c\\ b&a\end{pmatrix}.
\]

The group formed by all substitutions of this form according to (14) will be denoted by $\mathfrak L$ (the group of linear substitutions).

The functions (12) of $z'$, and hence also the substitutions (13), remain unchanged if the four numbers $a,b,c,\delta$ are all multiplied by one and the same factor not divisible by $p$. Consequently, by (10), the number of these functions, or the degree of the group $\mathfrak L$, is
\begin{equation}
 p(p^2-1),
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
which is also easily found by direct counting.

If in one of the substitutions (13) the four numbers $a,b,c,\delta$ are multiplied by a common factor not divisible by $p$, then the determinant $\Delta$ is multiplied by the square of this factor. Hence it is not the determinant $\Delta$ which is preserved by this multiplication, but rather its quadratic character, that is, the value of the symbol
\[
 \left({\Delta\over p}\right).
\]

If two of the substitutions (13) are composed, their determinants multiply, and from this it follows that all substitutions of the group $\mathfrak L$ in which $\Delta$ is a quadratic residue of $p$ form a group among themselves, which we shall denote by $\mathfrak L_0$.

The group $\mathfrak L_0$ is a proper divisor of the group $\mathfrak L$ of index $2$.

If the substitutions of $\mathfrak L_0$ are composed with any substitution $(z,\beta z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\beta^{-1}\end{pmatrix}$, where $\beta$ and hence also $\beta^{-1}$ is a quadratic non-residue of $p$, then the whole group $\mathfrak L$ is obtained.

If $\Delta$ is a quadratic residue of $p$, then a common factor to be adjoined to $a,b,c,\delta$ can be chosen so that $\Delta=1\pmod p$, and hence we can represent the group $\mathfrak L_0$ also by
\begin{equation}
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=1\pmod p.
\tag{16}
\end{equation}
In one of these substitutions the numbers $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ are determined modulo $p$ up to the common sign. The degree of the group $\mathfrak L_0$ is
\begin{equation}
 {1\over2}p(p^2-1).
\tag{17}
\end{equation}

But one comes directly to the form (16) if, as group of the division equation, one considers not the group $\mathfrak U$, but the group $\mathfrak B$ of \S~63, that is, if the $p$th roots of unity are adjoined to the rationality domain, whence the theorem follows:

The group $\mathfrak L_0$ is the group of the transformation equation when the $p$th roots of unity are adjoined to the rationality domain.

For the reduction of the group $\mathfrak L$ to the group $\mathfrak L_0$, however, the adjunction of a two-valued function is already sufficient; thus the adjunction of the $p$th roots of unity does too much. The theorems of \S\S~63, 65 serve to recognize which irrationality must be adjoined.

In \S~63, 3., we saw that the $p$th root of unity $\varrho$ is rationally representable by the roots of the division equation, and that by a substitution of the group $\mathfrak U$ whose determinant is congruent to $m$, $\varrho$ passes into $\varrho^m$; further, in \S~65 we proved that by adjoining all roots of a transformation equation the group of the division equation is reduced to the group $\mathfrak U_0$, which consists of all substitutions of the form
\begin{equation}
\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\0&a\end{pmatrix},
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
where $a$ is an arbitrary number not divisible by $p$. The determinants of the substitutions of $\mathfrak U_0$ are therefore squares and hence are congruent modulo $p$ to one of the ${1\over2}(p-1)$ quadratic residues of $p$.

The sum
\begin{equation}
 A=\sum^a \varrho^a,
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
where $a$ is to run through all quadratic residues of $p$, therefore remains unchanged under the substitutions of $\mathfrak U_0$ and consequently can be expressed rationally by the roots of the transformation equation. The sum $A$ remains unchanged under the substitutions of the group $\mathfrak B$ and hence also under $\mathfrak L_0$, while under the substitutions of $\mathfrak L$, and hence also of $\mathfrak U$, it receives two different values, namely, if $b$ runs through the series of non-residues,
\[
 A=\sum^a\varrho^a,
 \qquad
 B=\sum^b\varrho^b.
\]
Therefore $A$ is a function belonging to the group $\mathfrak L_0$, and by its adjunction $\mathfrak L$ is reduced to $\mathfrak L_0$.

The values of the sums $A,B$ are known, however (Vol. I, \S~179):
\[
 A={-1+\sqrt{(-1)^{(p-1)/2}p}\over2},
 \qquad
 B={-1-\sqrt{(-1)^{(p-1)/2}p}\over2}.
\]
The sign of the root depends on the choice of the root $\varrho$ and can be determined, but this is not relevant here. We therefore have the theorem:

The group of the transformation equation is $\mathfrak L_0$ when $\sqrt{(-1)^{(p-1)/2}p}$ is adjoined to the rationality domain.

The group of the invariant equation can also be derived without division of the elliptic functions in the following simple way, though in this way one obtains only the monodromy group, that is, the group in the field of rational functions of $j(\omega)$, without regard to the constants to be adjoined. We restrict ourselves to the case of a prime degree $p$ of the transformation, and put
\begin{equation}
 v_c=j\left({c+\omega\over p}\right),
 \qquad
 v_\infty=j(p\omega),
 \qquad
 u=j(\omega),
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
where $c$ is to be taken modulo $p$.

If a linear substitution
\[
 S=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\]
is applied to $\omega$, then $j(\omega)$ remains unchanged and $v_c$ passes into $v_{c'}$. To find $c'$, one has to seek a second linear substitution
\[
 S'=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\ \gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}
\]
such that
\[
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\ \gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\ c'&1\end{pmatrix}
=
\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\ c&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\]
or
\[
 p\alpha'+c'\beta'=p\alpha,
 \qquad
 \beta'=p\beta,
\]
\[
 p\gamma'+c'\delta'=c\alpha+\gamma,
 \qquad
 \delta'=c\beta+\delta.
\]
From this, by the third equation, $c'\delta'=c\alpha+\gamma$; and with the help of the fourth,
\[
 c'={c\alpha+\gamma\over c\beta+\delta}\pmod p.
\]
If $c\beta+\delta=0$, then $c'=\infty$; and if $c=\infty$, then $c'=\alpha/\beta$.

In applying this to the determination of $c,c'$, one may replace $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ by congruent numbers $a,b,c,d$ whose determinant $ad-bc$ is a quadratic residue of $p$, and it can also be shown that in this way every substitution of the group $\mathfrak L_0$ can be obtained. Thus every function of $v$ which remains unchanged under the substitutions of the group $\mathfrak L_0$ also remains unchanged when a linear substitution is applied to $\omega$, and is consequently a rational function of $j(\omega)$. But as to the nature of the coefficients in this function, this consideration teaches us nothing, and hence $\mathfrak L_0$ is recognized only as the monodromy group of the invariant equation.

\clearpage
\section*{Volume III. Eighth Section. The group of the transformation equations and the equation of the fifth degree.}

\section*{\S~79. Examination of the group $\Lzero$.}

In the tenth section of Volume II we investigated the congruence group and its divisors quite generally. Here we carry out this investigation once more in a special form, insofar as it has reference to the transformation problem.

The substitutions contained in $\Lzero$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}a&b\\ c&d\end{pmatrix}
 =\left(z,{ -c+az\over d-bz}\right),
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
in which
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=ad-bc
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
is a quadratic residue of $p$, can, as already remarked above, be brought to the form
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
 =\left(z,{ -\gamma+\alpha z\over \delta-\beta z}\right),
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=1\pmod p.
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
For we need only, if by $\sqrt\Delta$ (mod $p$) we understand a whole number whose square is congruent to $\Delta$ modulo $p$, to put
\[
 a\equiv \alpha\sqrt\Delta,
 \quad b\equiv\beta\sqrt\Delta,
 \quad c\equiv\gamma\sqrt\Delta,
 \quad d\equiv\delta\sqrt\Delta\pmod p.
\]
In the form (3) the signs of $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ can still be changed simultaneously.

We ask for those elements $z$ which remain unchanged by a substitution of the form (3). These are determined by the congruence
\[
 z\equiv {-\gamma+\alpha z\over \delta-\beta z}\pmod p,
\]
or
\begin{equation}
 \beta z^2+(\alpha-\delta)z-\gamma\equiv0\pmod p,
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
a congruence which, unless it is identical, has at most two incongruent roots. To obtain these, under the supposition first that $\beta$ is not divisible by $p$, we write (5) as
\[
 \left(\beta z+{\alpha-\delta\over2}\right)^2
 \equiv \left({\alpha-\delta\over2}\right)^2+\beta\gamma\pmod p,
\]
or, with the help of (4),
\begin{equation}
 \left(\beta z+{\alpha-\delta\over2}\right)^2
 \equiv \left({\alpha+\delta\over2}\right)^2-1\pmod p.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
Accordingly three different cases are to be distinguished:
\[
\text{I.}\qquad
 \left({\alpha+\delta\over2}\right)^2-1\equiv0\pmod p;
\]
then the congruence (5) has one root; there is one and only one element $z$ which remains unchanged.
\[
\text{II.}\qquad
 \left({\alpha+\delta\over2}\right)^2-1
\quad\text{is a quadratic residue of }p;
\]
the congruence (5) has two different roots; there are two elements $z$ which remain unchanged.
\[
\text{III.}\qquad
 \left({\alpha+\delta\over2}\right)^2-1
\quad\text{is a quadratic non-residue of }p;
\]
the congruence (5) has no root at all, and all elements $z$ are permuted.

If $\beta\equiv0$, then (5) always has the one root $z=\infty$; if then $\delta=\alpha$ and $\gamma\not\equiv0\pmod p$, there is only this one. But in this case
\[
 \alpha\delta\equiv1,
 \quad \alpha\equiv\delta,
 \quad \left({\alpha+\delta\over2}\right)^2\equiv1\pmod p,
\]
and condition I is fulfilled. If however at the same time $\gamma\equiv0\pmod p$, the substitution is the identical one.

If $\beta\equiv0$, but $\alpha-\delta\not\equiv0\pmod p$, then (5) has still a second root; since now $\alpha\delta\equiv1\pmod p$, we have
\[
 \left({\alpha+\delta\over2}\right)^2-1
 \equiv \left({\alpha-\delta\over2}\right)^2\pmod p,
\]
therefore a quadratic residue, and condition II is fulfilled. We summarize these propositions thus:

In case I one element, or all elements, remain unchanged; in case II two elements remain unchanged; and in case III all elements are changed.

If we repeat one and the same substitution
\begin{equation}
 A=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
several times, the substitutions
\[
 A,A^2,A^3,\ldots
\]
arise, and in their series the identical substitution $\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ must occur once. If $n$ is the least positive number for which
\[
 A^n=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
then $n$ is called the degree of $A$ (Vol. II, \S~2).

If for an arbitrary $m$ we put
\[
 A^m=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_m&\beta_m\\\gamma_m&\delta_m\end{pmatrix},
\]
then for the computation of the numbers $\alpha_m,\beta_m,\gamma_m,\delta_m$ we obtain the following system of recurrent formulae:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \alpha_{m+1}&=\alpha\alpha_m+\beta\gamma_m,&
 \beta_{m+1}&=\alpha\beta_m+\beta\delta_m,\\
 \gamma_{m+1}&=\gamma\alpha_m+\delta\gamma_m,&
 \delta_{m+1}&=\gamma\beta_m+\delta\delta_m.
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
The numbers $\alpha_m,\beta_m,\gamma_m,\delta_m$ are computed especially simply from these formulae in case I.

In this case, since the signs of $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ may all be simultaneously reversed, we can assume
\[
 \alpha+
\delta=2,
 \qquad \alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=1\pmod p,
\]
and from (8) obtain
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
\alpha_2\equiv -1+2\alpha,&\beta_2\equiv2\beta,\\
\delta_2\equiv -1+2\delta,&\gamma_2\equiv2\gamma,\\
\alpha_3\equiv -2+3\alpha,&\beta_3\equiv3\beta,\\
\delta_3\equiv -2+3\delta,&\gamma_3\equiv3\gamma,
\end{array}\pmod p,
\]
from which, by the induction from $m$ to $m+1$, it follows that
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_m\equiv-(m-1)+m\alpha,
 \quad \delta_m\equiv-(m-1)+m\delta,
 \quad \beta_m\equiv m\beta,
 \quad \gamma_m\equiv m\gamma\pmod p.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
From this one sees that all the $A^m$ again belong to case I, and that $p$ is the degree of $A$.

The analogous consideration of the two other cases has been carried out by Serret, but appears unnecessary for our purpose.

On the other hand, we shall add here the theorem that the whole group $\Lzero$ can be composed from the following two special substitutions
\begin{equation}
 A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
The proof follows from \S~50, if one observes that to each number system $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ satisfying the condition $\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=1\pmod p$, the number system $\alpha',\beta',\gamma',\delta'$ can be chosen so that
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\\gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}
 \equiv
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
 \pmod p,
 \qquad
 \alpha'\delta'-\beta'\gamma'=1.
\]
Thus all substitutions of $\Lzero$ are contained among those considered in \S~30. But we can also here easily display the composition of an arbitrary substitution in $\Lzero$ actually from $A$ and $B$, and so give the proof independently of \S~30.

For if first $c$ is an arbitrary number not divisible by $p$, then actual calculation gives easily
\begin{equation}
 A^c=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ c&1\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 C=\begin{pmatrix}c&0\\0&c^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 =A^{-c^{-1}}BA^{-c}BA^{-c^{-1}}B,
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
and then, if $\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}$ is any substitution in $\Lzero$ and $\beta$ is different from $0$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\ \delta\beta^{-1}-1&1\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\beta&0\\0&\beta^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \alpha\beta^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix},
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
and if $\beta=0$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\\gamma&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\0&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\\gamma\alpha&1\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
It is to be concluded from this theorem that a group contained in $\Lzero$ which contains the two substitutions $A,B$ must necessarily be identical with $\Lzero$.

\section*{\S~80. Normal divisors of the group $\Lzero$.}

In order to learn the possible reductions of the transformation equation, it is above all necessary to investigate the divisors of the group $\Lzero$. We ask first about the existence of a normal divisor $\Kgrp$ of $\Lzero$ (Vol. II, \S~3).\footnote{According to Galois: ``proper divisors''.}

Let
\begin{equation}
 S=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
be any substitution in $\Kgrp$ different from the identical substitution. Then, by the nature of a normal divisor, every substitution
\begin{equation}
 U=TST^{-1}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
is also contained in $\Kgrp$, if
\begin{equation}
 T=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\\gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
is an arbitrary substitution in $\Lzero$.

We set ourselves the problem of determining $T$ and $\xi$ so that
\begin{equation}
 U=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi\end{pmatrix}
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
shall hold. This condition can also be written
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\\gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha'&\beta'\\\gamma'&\delta'\end{pmatrix},
\]
and leads to the congruences
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
1.&\alpha'\alpha+\beta'\gamma&\equiv\gamma',\\
2.&\alpha'\beta+\beta'\delta&\equiv\delta',\\
3.&\gamma'\alpha+\delta'\gamma&\equiv-\alpha'+\gamma'\xi,\\
4.&\gamma'\beta+\delta'\delta&\equiv-\beta'+\delta'\xi,
\end{array}\pmod p,
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
and from 1 and 2 there also follows
\begin{equation}
 \alpha'\equiv\gamma'\delta-\delta'\gamma,
 \qquad
 \beta'\equiv-\gamma'\beta+\delta'\alpha\pmod p.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
If these values are inserted in the third and fourth congruences of (5), there follows
\[
 \gamma'(\alpha+\delta-\xi)\equiv0,
 \qquad
 \delta'(\alpha+\delta-\xi)\equiv0\pmod p,
\]
whence, since $\gamma',\delta'$ cannot both be divisible by $p$, it follows that
\begin{equation}
 \xi\equiv\alpha+
\delta\pmod p.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
If $\xi$ is so determined, then in (5) the congruences 3 and 4 follow from 1 and 2. If one now inserts $\alpha',\beta'$ from 1 and 2 in
\begin{equation}
 \alpha'\delta'-\beta'\gamma'\equiv1\pmod p,
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \alpha'^2\beta+\alpha'\beta'(\delta-\alpha)-\beta'^2\gamma\equiv1\pmod p.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
If $\alpha',\beta'$ are determined from this, all demands stated in (5), and hence also in (2), (4), are satisfied.

It remains therefore only to prove that the congruence (9) is always soluble.

This possibility is evident if $\beta$ or $-\gamma$ is a quadratic residue of $p$; for then
\[
 \alpha'\equiv\sqrt{\beta^{-1}},\quad \beta'=0,
\]
or
\[
 \alpha'=0,
 \quad \beta'\equiv\sqrt{-\gamma^{-1}}\pmod p
\]
satisfies the demand.

It is further to be distinguished whether the substitution $S$ belongs to class I, II, or III of the preceding paragraph.

First, if
\[
\text{I.}\qquad
 \left({\alpha+
\delta\over2}\right)^2\equiv1\pmod p,
\]
then, if $S^m$ is put in place of $S$, by \S~79, (9), $\beta$ passes into $m\beta$ and $\gamma$ into $m\gamma$; and $m$ can always be so determined that $m\beta$ or $-m\gamma$ becomes a quadratic residue of $p$. This also includes the case in which $\beta=0$ or $\gamma=0$ and $\alpha\equiv\delta\pmod p$.

If, on the other hand, $\beta=0$ and $\alpha-\delta$ is not divisible by $p$, then one can choose $\beta'$ arbitrarily in (9) and then determine $\alpha'$ from a congruence of the first degree.

If $\beta$ is not divisible by $p$, put (9) in the form
\begin{equation}
 \left[\alpha'\beta+\beta'{\delta-\alpha\over2}\right]^2
 \equiv
 \beta+\beta'^2\left[\left({\alpha+
\delta\over2}\right)^2-1\right]
 \pmod p.
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
If now
\[
\text{II.}\qquad
 \left({\alpha+
\delta\over2}\right)^2-1
\quad\text{is a quadratic residue of }p,
\]
then $\beta'$ is determined from the congruence
\[
 \beta'^2\left[\left({\alpha+
\delta\over2}\right)^2-1\right]
 \equiv \left({\beta-1\over2}\right)^2,
\]
whereby (10) passes into
\[
 \alpha'\beta+
