\beta'{\delta-\alpha\over2}\equiv
 \pm {\beta+1\over2}\pmod p,
\]
and from this $\alpha'$ can be determined, whichever sign is chosen.

If
\[
\text{III.}\qquad
 \left({\alpha+
\delta\over2}\right)^2-1
\quad\text{is a quadratic non-residue of }p,
\]
and at the same time $\beta$ is a quadratic non-residue, then a non-residue $\nu$ can always be so determined that $\beta+\nu$ becomes a quadratic residue; for if $\nu$ in $\beta+\nu$ is allowed to run through the series of non-residues, not all non-residues can arise, since among them $\beta$ too would have to occur.

Then $\beta'$ can be so determined that
\[
 \beta'^2\left[\left({\alpha+
\delta\over2}\right)^2-1\right]
 \equiv \nu\pmod p,
\]
and then $\alpha'$ from
\[
 \alpha'\beta+
\beta'{\delta-\alpha\over2}\equiv
 \pm\sqrt{\beta+
\nu}\pmod p.
\]
From this follows:

In a normal divisor $\Kgrp$ of the group $\Lzero$ there must certainly occur a substitution $U$ of the form
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
Let first $\xi$ be different from zero modulo $p$.

By the concept of the normal divisor, $\Kgrp$ also contains the substitution
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}\xi&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
and consequently also
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\xi&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\ \xi^2-1&-1\end{pmatrix}
 =A^{\xi^2-1}\quad[\S~79,(11)],
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
hence also $A$ and all its powers. Therefore $\Kgrp$ also contains
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \eta&1\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&\xi+
\eta\end{pmatrix}
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
for arbitrary $\eta$, that is, the substitution (11) for every arbitrary $\xi$, and hence also
\[
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Accordingly, by the theorem of the preceding paragraph, the group $\Kgrp$ is identical with $\Lzero$.

There remains still the possibility to discuss in which, in (11), $\xi=0$.

In this case the group $\Kgrp$ contains the substitution
\[
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},
\]
and consequently, for an arbitrary $\alpha$ not divisible by $p$, also
\[
 V=\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha\\-\alpha^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&-\alpha\\\alpha^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^2&0\\0&\alpha^{-2}\end{pmatrix}.
\]
From this one derives further, as contained in $\Kgrp$,
\[
 W=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{-2}&0\\0&\alpha^2\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha^2&0\\0&\alpha^{-2}\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \alpha^4-1&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
If now $p$ is greater than $5$, then $\alpha$ can be so taken that $\alpha^4-1$ is not divisible by $p$; then $\Kgrp$ also contains the substitution $A$ and its powers, and hence $\Kgrp$ is identical with $\Lzero$.

If $p=5$, then $\alpha^4-1$ is always divisible by $p$, so this conclusion is not applicable. But in this case it follows from the concept of the normal divisor that the following is contained in $\Kgrp$:
\[
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\2&-2\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}-2&-1\\-2&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}-2&0\\0&2\end{pmatrix},
\]
\[
\begin{pmatrix}-2&0\\0&2\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&-2\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\2&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
that is, $A^2$, and hence all powers of $A$. From this one concludes as above that $\Kgrp$ is identical with $\Lzero$. We have therefore the theorem:

If $p>3$, the group $\Lzero$ has no normal divisor.

In the case $p=3$, $\Kgrp$ likewise contains
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},
\]
and therefore also
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\pm1&0\\\pm1&1\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\\mp1&1\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}\mp1&1\\1&\pm1\end{pmatrix},
\]
and in fact the four substitutions
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix}
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
form a normal divisor of $\Lzero$ of index $3$.

In the case $p=3$, the group $\Lzero$ is isomorphic with the alternating group of interchanges of four elements. This is the group of an arbitrary equation of the fourth degree when the square root of the discriminant is adjoined to the domain of rationality. The divisor (15) of this group of index $3$ then supplies the cubic resolvent of the biquadratic equation known in algebra.

A function belonging to the group (15) of the roots $v_\infty,v_0,v_1,v_2$ of the transformation equation for the third transformation degree is, for example,
\[
 v_\infty v_0+v_1v_2
\]
or
\[
 (v_\infty-v_0)(v_1-v_2).
\]
The difference between these two functions is that the first remains unchanged by the substitution of determinant $-1$
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
whereas the second changes its sign under it. The first will therefore lead to an equation of the third degree with rational coefficients, while in the cubic equation for the second $\sqrt{-3}$ still occurs (\S~78).

\clearpage
\section*{Volume III. Eighth Section. The group of the transformation equations and the equation of the fifth degree.}

\section*{\S~81. Non-normal divisors of $\Lzero$.}

Leaving aside the case $p=3$, we now ask for the non-normal divisors of the group $\Lzero$ whose index is smaller than $p+1$; on the existence of such divisors depends the possibility of forming resolvents of the transformation equation whose degree is lower than $p+1$.

First it can be shown that the index of a divisor $\Kgrp$ of $\Lzero$ can never be smaller than $p$.

Let
\begin{equation}
 \Kgrp=S_0,S_1,\ldots,S_{\nu-1}
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
be any divisor of $\Lzero$ of degree $\nu$, and, if it is possible,
\begin{equation}
 T=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
a substitution of degree $p$ which occurs in $\Lzero$ but not in $\Kgrp$. Then, since $p$ is prime, no lower power of $T$ than the $p$th can occur in $\Kgrp$. The cosets
\begin{equation}
 \Kgrp,\quad T\Kgrp,\quad T^2\Kgrp,\ldots,\quad T^{p-1}\Kgrp
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
contain altogether mutually different elements $T^iS_k$, and hence $\nu p$ is at most equal to the degree of the group $\Lzero$, that is:
\[
 \nu p\leq p\,{p^2-1\over2}.
\]
Thus the index of $\Kgrp$, i.e. the quotient
\[
 p\,{p^2-1\over2}:\nu,
\]
is equal to $p$ or greater than $p$.

A divisor $\Kgrp$ of $\Lzero$ whose index is smaller than $p$ must therefore contain all substitutions of degree $p$, hence also (\S~79, I) all substitutions $T$ in which
\begin{equation}
 \alpha+\delta\equiv2\pmod p
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
holds. Consequently such a group $\Kgrp$ contains first the substitution
\begin{equation}
 A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
and its powers; likewise the substitutions
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},
\]
and hence also
\begin{equation}
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
But if the substitutions $A,B$ are contained in $\Kgrp$, then by \S~79 $\Kgrp$ must be identical with $\Lzero$. We therefore have the theorem:

The index of a divisor of $\Lzero$ cannot be smaller than $p$.

Our problem is therefore restricted to the question of the divisors of $\Lzero$ of index $p$, or of degree
\[
 {p^2-1\over2}.
\]
Let $\Kgrp$ be such a divisor and let $S_0,S_1,\ldots,S_{\nu-1}$ be its elements, so that the number $\nu$ has the value
\begin{equation}
 \nu={p^2-1\over2}.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}

Since the degree of every element of a group is always a divisor of the degree of the group, no element of degree $p$ can occur in $\Kgrp$, since $\nu$ is not divisible by $p$. If therefore for $T$ we take the substitution of degree $p$
\begin{equation}
 A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
then the group $\Lzero$ can be decomposed into the $p$ cosets:
\begin{equation}
 \Lzero=\Kgrp,\quad A\Kgrp,\quad A^2\Kgrp,\ldots,\quad A^{p-1}\Kgrp.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

Together with $\Kgrp$, all its conjugate divisors
\[
 T\Kgrp T^{-1}
\]
have the same degree and hence also the same index. Let now $g$ be a primitive root of the prime number $p$, and
\begin{equation}
 C=\begin{pmatrix}g&0\\0&g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
a substitution which plainly has degree $(p-1)/2$. Like every substitution of $\Lzero$, it must occur in one of the rows (9); that is, there is a substitution $S$ in $\Kgrp$ and an exponent $\lambda$ for which
\begin{equation}
 C=A^\lambda S,\qquad S=A^{-\lambda}C.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
But $\gamma$ can further be determined so that
\begin{equation}
 A^\gamma C A^{-\gamma}=C;
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
for this one obtains the condition
\[
 \gamma(g-g^{-1})\equiv\lambda g\pmod p,
\]
which, since $p>3$, can always be satisfied; but then, by (11),
\[
 C=A^\gamma S A^{-\gamma}.
\]
Thus $C$ occurs in the divisor $A^\gamma\Kgrp A^{-\gamma}$ conjugate to $\Kgrp$, and therefore, by putting this group in place of $\Kgrp$, we may assume without loss of generality that $\Kgrp$ itself contains the substitution $C$.

Now in $\Kgrp$ the substitution
\[
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
\]
may either occur or not occur. In the first case $\Kgrp$ also contains all substitutions composed from $B$ and $C$, which are all of one of the two forms
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}g^r&0\\0&g^{-r}\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&g^r\\-g^{-r}&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad r=0,1,\ldots,{p-3\over2},
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
and their number is $p-1$. Thus in the first case $\Kgrp$ contains all substitutions of the forms (13), (14), and in the second all substitutions of the form (13) and none of the form (14).

Let now
\begin{equation}
 V=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
be a substitution contained in $\Kgrp$ which is contained neither in the form (13) nor in the form (14), so that neither $\alpha$ and $\delta$, nor $\beta$ and $\gamma$, are simultaneously congruent to $0$. Then all substitutions of the form
\begin{equation}
 C^rVC^s=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \alpha g^{r+s} & \beta g^{r-s}\\
 \gamma g^{-r+s} & \delta g^{-r-s}
 \end{pmatrix},
\tag{16}
\end{equation}
when $r,s$ both run through the row of numbers
\[
 0,1,2,\ldots,{p-3\over2},
\]
are contained in $\Kgrp$ and are all distinct from one another. For if two of the substitutions (16) were equal, then there would have to be one among them which, without $r,s$ vanishing, is identical with $V$. This requires
\[
 \alpha=\pm\alpha g^{r+s},\quad
 \beta=\pm\beta g^{r-s},\quad
 \gamma=\pm\gamma g^{-r+s},\quad
 \delta=\pm\delta g^{-r-s}\pmod p,
\]
where in all four formulae either the upper or the lower signs hold; hence, since neither $\alpha$ and $\delta$, nor $\beta$ and $\gamma$, vanish simultaneously,
\[
 r+s\equiv0,\qquad r-s\equiv0\pmod{p-1},
\]
or
\[
 r+s\equiv{p-1\over2},\qquad r-s\equiv{p-1\over2}\pmod{p-1},
\]
and therefore in both cases
\[
 r=0,\quad s=0\pmod{{p-1\over2}},\qquad\text{as was required to be proved.}
\]

In the form (16) there are therefore $\left({p-1\over2}\right)^2$ different substitutions. If the group $\Kgrp$ is not yet exhausted by these, one chooses a substitution $V'$ not contained in (13), (14), (16), and forms in the same way the row
\begin{equation}
 C^rV'C^s,
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
whose substitutions are different both among themselves and from those contained in (13), (14), (16), and one continues in this way until the whole group $\Kgrp$ is exhausted.

If the number of rows so formed, (16), (17), \ldots, is denoted by $q$, then the degree of the group $\Kgrp$ is obtained as follows:

1. if $B$ does not occur in $\Kgrp$:
\[
 ={p-1\over2}+q\left({p-1\over2}\right)^2;
\]
2. if $B$ occurs in $\Kgrp$:
\[
 =p-1+q\left({p-1\over2}\right)^2.
\]
This number, according to the requirement of our problem, must be
\[
 ={(p-1)(p+1)\over2}.
\]
But from this it follows that case 1 is impossible; for in that case one would have to have
\[
 q\left({p-1\over2}\right)=p,
\]
hence
\[
 q=p,\qquad p=3,
\]
which we have excluded.

In case 2, however, it follows for every arbitrary $p$ that
\[
 q=2.
\]
Hence in any case the substitution $B$ must occur in $\Kgrp$, and the group sought is exhausted by (13), (14), (16), (17).

We first show that in the two substitutions $V,V'$ of the group $\Kgrp$ none of the numbers $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\alpha',\beta',\gamma',\delta'$ can be congruent to $0$. Namely, as we have already seen, there does not occur in $\Kgrp$
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \gamma&1\end{pmatrix}
\]
if $\gamma$ is different from $0$, and therefore also not
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\0&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\ \alpha\gamma&1\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\ \gamma&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix},
\]
for arbitrary $\alpha$, and consequently also not
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\ \gamma&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha\\-\alpha^{-1}&\gamma\end{pmatrix},
\]
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\ \gamma&\alpha^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\alpha^{-1}\\-\alpha&0\end{pmatrix},
\]
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha\\-\alpha^{-1}&-\gamma\end{pmatrix}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&\gamma\\0&\alpha\end{pmatrix};
\]
among these are contained all substitutions
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
\]
in which one of the four numbers $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ is congruent to $0$.

We now denote the substitutions of the system (16) by
\[
 W=\begin{pmatrix}
 \alpha g^{r+s} & \beta g^{r-s}\\
 \gamma g^{-r+s} & \delta g^{-r-s}
 \end{pmatrix},
\]
and put
\begin{equation}
 \xi\equiv\alpha g^{r+s},\qquad \varrho\equiv\alpha^{-1}\beta g^{-2s}\pmod p,
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \alpha\delta\equiv m,\qquad \beta\gamma\equiv m-1.
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
The number $m$ does not change when $V$ is replaced by an arbitrary substitution of the system (16). The number $m'$ is to have the corresponding meaning for the system (17); in either of the two systems $\xi$ can assume each of the values
\begin{equation}
 1,2,\ldots,p-1
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
and $\varrho$ runs through, according as $\alpha^{-1}\beta$ is a quadratic residue or non-residue, in one system the row of residues or that of non-residues. Changing the sign of $\xi$ gives no new substitution $W$. Accordingly we can represent $W$ in this way:
\begin{equation}
 W=\begin{pmatrix}
 \xi & \xi\varrho\\
 \xi^{-1}\varrho^{-1}(m-1) & \xi^{-1}m
 \end{pmatrix}.
\tag{21}
\end{equation}

Since now the square of $W$,
\[
 W^2=\begin{pmatrix}
 \xi^2+m-1 & (\xi^2+m)\varrho\\
 \xi^{-2}\varrho^{-1}(m-1)(\xi^2+m) &
 [(m-1)\xi^2+m^2]\xi^{-2}
 \end{pmatrix},
\]
must belong to $\Kgrp$, it is contained under one of the systems (13), (14), (16), (17), from which the following four possibilities result:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{ll}
1.& \xi^2+m\equiv0,\\
2.& \xi^2+m-1\equiv0,\quad (m-1)\xi^2+m^2\equiv0,\\
3.& (\xi^2+m-1)[(m-1)\xi^2+m^2]\equiv m'\xi^2,\\
4.& (\xi^2+m-1)[(m-1)\xi^2+m^2]\equiv m\xi^2,
\end{array}
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
and each of the $p-1$ values (20), inserted for $\xi$, must satisfy one of these four cases. If one of the congruences (22), 2, is fulfilled, then the other must follow from it, which is possible only when
\begin{equation}
 2m\equiv1,\qquad \xi^2\equiv m\pmod p.
\tag{23}
\end{equation}

Now a congruence with respect to a prime modulus has at most as many incongruent roots as the degree of the congruence; 1 and 2 can therefore be satisfied for at most two values each, and 3 and 4 for at most four values each, of $\xi$. Thus altogether there are at most twelve values of $\xi$ satisfying one of the four congruences (22).

It follows that
\[
 p-1\leq12,\qquad p\leq13.
\]
But if $p=13$, then each of the congruences (22) must have the maximum number of roots; hence 2 must also be satisfied for two values of $\xi$; then by (23) it would have to be that $m\equiv7$, $\xi^2\equiv7$ (mod 13), which is impossible, since 7 is a quadratic non-residue of 13.

A divisor of $\Lzero$ of index $p$ therefore does not exist when $p>11$.

\section*{\S~82. Divisors of $\Lzero$ of index $p$ for $p=5,7,11$.}

There remains the possibility that for $p=5,7,11$ there exist divisors of $\Lzero$ of index $p$.

1. $p=5$. We first investigate the case $p=5$. Since $m$ and $m-1$ cannot be congruent to $0$, the assumptions for $m$ that remain are
\[
 m\equiv2,3,4\pmod p.
\]
But if we replace the substitution $W$ of \S~81, (21) by
\begin{equation}
 WB=\begin{pmatrix}
 -\xi\varrho & \xi\\
 -\xi^{-1}m & \xi^{-1}\varrho^{-1}(m-1)
 \end{pmatrix},
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
whereby $m$ passes into $1-m$, then the case $m\equiv4$ is reduced to the case $m\equiv2$.

For $m\equiv2$, however, (23) of \S~81 is not fulfilled, and none of the congruences (22) is possible, since $\pm2$ are non-residues of 5 and the left-hand sides of (22), 3 and 4, reduce for $m=2$ to $\xi^4-1$, which is divisible by 5 for every non-zero $\xi$. Thus only this remains:
\begin{equation}
 m\equiv3,\qquad m'\equiv3\pmod5,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
and the two systems $W,W'$ [\S~81, (16), (17)] can differ from one another only in that $\alpha^{-1}\beta$, and hence also $\varrho$, is in one a quadratic residue and in the other a quadratic non-residue of 5. The group sought therefore consists, if it exists, of the 12 substitutions contained in the three forms
\begin{equation}
 \begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\0&\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\quad
 \begin{pmatrix}0&\xi\\-\xi^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
 \begin{pmatrix}\xi&\eta\\2\eta^{-1}&3\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
where one is to put
\[
 \xi\equiv1,2,\qquad \eta\equiv\pm1,\pm2\pmod5.
\]

2. $p=7,11$.

Since $-1$ for $p=7,11$ is to be found among the quadratic non-residues, the composed substitution $WB$, (1), belongs to the $W'$ [because the quadratic character of the quantity denoted in (18), \S~81 by $\varrho$ is opposite in $W$ and $WB$]. Accordingly, by (1) and \S~81, (19), (21),
\[
 \beta'\gamma'\equiv -m\equiv m'-1,
\]
hence
\begin{equation}
 m+m'\equiv1\pmod p.
\tag{4}
\end{equation}

For $p=7$, since interchanging $m$ and $m'$ gives nothing new, the three following possibilities remain:
\[
 1.\ m\equiv2,\ m'\equiv6;\qquad
 2.\ m\equiv3,\ m'\equiv5;\qquad
 3.\ m\equiv4,\ m'\equiv4.
\]
In case 1, of the congruences \S~81, (22), 1 and 2 are impossible; hence for every $\xi$ one of the two congruences 3, 4 would have to be fulfilled, which here read
\[
 (\xi^2+1)(\xi^2+4)\equiv2\xi^2,\ 6\xi^2\pmod7;
\]
neither of these is fulfilled for $\xi\equiv1$.

In case 3, (22), 1 is not satisfiable and (22), 2 is fulfilled for $\xi^2\equiv4$; hence for $\xi=1,2$ the congruence (22), 3 or 4,
\[
 (\xi^2+3)(3\xi^2+2)\equiv4\xi^2\pmod7
\]
would have to be fulfilled, which again is not the case. Thus for $p=7$ there remains only
\begin{equation}
 m\equiv3,\qquad m'\equiv5\pmod7.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}

For $p=11$, the possibility is excluded from the outset that the congruences (22), 2, be fulfilled; for in that case, in consequence of (4) and \S~81, (23),
\[
 m\equiv m'
\]
would have to hold; then (22), 3 and 4 would not be different, and the congruences (22) together could have at most eight roots and not ten, as they would have to. Thus the congruences (22), 1, 3, 4 must each have the maximum number of roots, and, in order that 1 be fulfilled, $m$, and for the same reasons also $m'$, must be a quadratic non-residue of 11. But only the two numbers
\begin{equation}
 m,m'\equiv2,-1\pmod {11}
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
satisfy this condition and the condition (4) simultaneously.

The groups sought therefore consist in these two cases, if they exist, of the following substitutions:
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
p=7:&
\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\0&\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\
\begin{pmatrix}0&\xi\\ \xi^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},\
\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\varrho\\2\xi^{-1}\varrho^{-1}&3\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\
\begin{pmatrix}-\xi\varrho&\xi\\-3\xi^{-1}&2\xi^{-1}\varrho^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\xi\equiv1,2,3\pmod7,
\end{array}
\tag{7}
\]
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
p=11:&
\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\0&\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\
\begin{pmatrix}0&\xi\\-\xi^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},\
\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\varrho\\\xi^{-1}\varrho^{-1}&2\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\
\begin{pmatrix}-\xi\varrho&\xi\\-2\xi^{-1}&\xi^{-1}\varrho^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\xi\equiv1,2,3,4,5\pmod {11};
\end{array}
\tag{8}
\]
in (7) and (8) $\varrho$ runs either through the row of quadratic residues or through that of non-residues, so that for $p=7$ or $11$ one obtains two groups of index $p$ in each case.

That the substitution-systems assembled in (3), (7), (8) really constitute groups can be shown by direct composition in several ways. More simply, however, one reaches this proof by forming functions of the $v_z$, \S~78, (7), which remain unchanged by the substitutions of these systems and which, under the substitutions of $\Lzero$, acquire altogether only $p$ different values.

As is clear from their manner of origin, the systems (3), (7), (8) can be composed by repeated application of $B,C,U,U'$, when $U,U'$ are any two special substitutions $W,W'$. For $p=5$, $U^2$ belongs to the $W'$, and for $p=7,11$, $UB$ belongs to the $W'$, so that $U'$ may still be omitted. If we choose $U$ arbitrarily and take for the primitive root $g$ in $C$, for $p=5,7,11$ respectively, $2,-2,2$, we can compose the group (3) from
\[
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
 C=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&3\end{pmatrix},\quad
 U=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\2&3\end{pmatrix}\quad(p=5),
\]
the group (7) from
\[
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
 C=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&4\end{pmatrix},\quad
 U=\begin{pmatrix}1&\pm1\\ \pm2&3\end{pmatrix}\quad(p=7),
\]
and the group (8) from
\[
 B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
 C=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&6\end{pmatrix},\quad
 U=\begin{pmatrix}1&\pm1\\ \pm1&2\end{pmatrix}\quad(p=11),
\]
where in the last two cases the double signs give rise to the two different groups mentioned above.

We now assemble the interchanges of the indices $z$ corresponding to these substitutions [\S~79, (3)].

For $p=5$:
\[
\begin{array}{rrrrrrr}
z=&\infty,&0,&1,&2,&3,&4,\\
B:&0,&\infty,&4,&2,&3,&1,\\
C:&\infty,&0,&4,&3,&2,&1,\\
U:&4,&1,&2,&0,&\infty,&3.
\end{array}
\]
Thus by $B,C,U$ the index-pairs
\[
 (\infty,0),\quad(1,4),\quad(2,3)
\]
are not torn apart, but only interchanged among one another. Moreover, in each case the elements in two of these pairs are interchanged. If therefore we form a function such as
\begin{equation}
 V=(v_\infty-v_0)(v_1-v_4)(v_2-v_3),
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
then it remains unchanged by $B,C,U$ and hence by the whole system (3), while by repeated application of the cyclic interchange $(0,1,2,3,4)$, that is, of the substitution
\[
 A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
it assumes five different values. But from $A$ and $B$ [\S~79, (10)] the whole group $\Lzero$ can be composed, and therefore $V$ obtains, under application of $\Lzero$, no more than five values. The five values of $V$ are the roots of an equation of the fifth degree.

For $p=7$ one has
\[
 B=\left(z,-{1\over z}\right),\qquad C=(z,4z),\qquad
 U=\left(z,{\mp2+z\over 3\mp z}\right).
\]
For better overview we further display $B,C,U$ by the cyclic interchanges that they produce in the eight values of $z$:
\[
\begin{aligned}
B&=(0,\infty)(1,-1)(2,3)(-2,-3),\\
C&=(1,-3,2)(-1,3,-2),\\
U&=(\infty,\mp1,\pm1,\pm3)(0,\mp3,\mp2,\pm2),
\end{aligned}
\]
and from this one obtains the seven-valued function
\begin{equation}
 V=(v_\infty-v_0)(v_{\mp1}-v_{\mp3})(v_{\pm1}-v_{\mp2})(v_{\pm3}-v_{\pm2}),
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
where one or the other sign may be taken.

For $p=11$ one has
\[
 B=\left(z,-{1\over z}\right),\qquad C=(z,4z),\qquad
 U=\left(z,{\mp1+z\over 2\mp z}\right),
\]
or, represented by cycles:
\[
\begin{aligned}
B&=(0,\infty)(1,-1)(2,5)(-2,-5)(3,-4)(-3,4),\\
C&=(1,4,5,-2,3)(-1,-4,-5,2,-3),\\
U&=(\infty,\mp1,\pm3,\mp2,\pm2)(0,\mp5,\mp5,\mp4,\pm1),
\end{aligned}
\]
from which arise the two eleven-valued functions
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 V={}&(v_\infty-v_0)(v_{\mp1}-v_{\mp5})(v_{\pm3}-v_{\mp5})\\
 &\times(v_{\mp2}-v_{\mp4})(v_{\pm2}-v_{\pm1})(v_{\pm4}-v_{\mp3}).
\end{aligned}
\tag{11}
\end{equation}

Here there remains the following remark of interest. The resolvents of degree $p$, whose roots are the quantities (9), (10), (11), still contain, according to \S~78, $\sqrt{\pm p}$ in their coefficients. But the group $\Lzero$ is extended to the group $\mathfrak L$ if we adjoin a linear substitution of the form \S~78, (13), in which $ad-bc$ is a quadratic non-residue, for instance
\[
 \begin{array}{ll}
 \text{for }p=5& (z,2z),\\[0.2em]
 \text{for }p=7,11& (z,-z).
 \end{array}
\]
By applying this substitution, however, the value (9)
\[
 V=(v_\infty-v_0)(v_1-v_4)(v_2-v_3)
\]
passes into its opposite, and from this it follows that, if $p=5$, $V^2$ satisfies an equation of the fifth degree with rational numerical coefficients. From this one concludes that in the equation for $V$ the coefficients of the odd powers have the factor $\sqrt5$, while the others are rational, or that for the unknown $\sqrt5\,V$ one obtains an equation with rational coefficients.

For $p=7,11$, the expressions corresponding to the two signs in (10) or (11) pass into one another through the interchange $(z,-z)$. The resolvents of degree 7 or 11 to which one of the two expressions (10), respectively (11), belongs pass into one another by changing the sign of $\sqrt{-7}$, respectively $\sqrt{-11}$.


\clearpage
\section*{\S\,83. Various resolvents of the fifth degree for the fifth transformation degree.}

In the formation of resolvents of degree \(p\) we restrict ourselves here to the case \(p=5\).

These resolvents can be given very manifold forms, in that in the function \(V\), (9) of the preceding paragraph, one may not only choose for the quantities \(v\) the roots of an arbitrary transformation equation, but may also replace \(V\) by many other functions, for example by
\[
 (v_\infty+v_0)(v_1+v_4)(v_2+v_3),
\]
or by
\[
 v_\infty v_0+v_1v_4+v_2v_3.
\]

We shall first apply the transformation equation \S~72, (8), in which now, in agreement with the preceding paragraph, \(v\) is put in place of \(x\):
\begin{equation}
 v^6+10v^3-\gamma_2 v+5=0.
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
Here, if
\[
 c\equiv0\pmod {12},\qquad z\equiv c\pmod5,
\]
then
\begin{equation}
 v_\infty=5\left({\eta(5\omega)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^2,\qquad
 v_z=\left({\eta\left({\omega+c\over5}\right)\over\eta(\omega)}\right)^2.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
If then we take
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \sqrt5\,w_z
 &= (v_\infty-v_z)(v_{z+1}-v_{z-1})(v_{z+2}-v_{z-2})\\
 &= (v_\infty-v_c)(v_{c+12}-v_{c-12})(v_{c+24}-v_{c-24}),\\
&\hspace{2em} z=0,1,2,3,4,\qquad c=0,\pm12,\pm24,
\end{aligned}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
then, by the concluding remark of the preceding paragraph, \(w_0,w_1,w_2,w_3,w_4\) are the roots of an equation of the fifth degree whose coefficients are formed rationally from \(\gamma_2\) and rational numbers.

If, however, one observes the relations [\S~54, (14)]
\[
 \gamma_2(\omega+1)=e^{-2\pi i/3}\gamma_2(\omega),
 \qquad
 \gamma_2\!\left(-{1\over\omega}\right)=\gamma_2(\omega),
\]
then from the form of (1) it follows that under the interchange \((\omega,\omega+1)\) the roots \(v\), apart from a rearrangement, acquire the factor \(e^{2\pi i/3}\), while under \((\omega,-1/\omega)\) they remain unchanged. Thus the \(w_z\) only interchange among themselves, and their symmetric functions remain unchanged and therefore depend rationally only on the invariant \(j(\omega)\). Moreover the coefficients in the equation for \(w\) can become infinite for no finite \(j(\omega)\), and consequently are integral functions of \(j(\omega)\).

A further indication about these coefficients is obtained from the expansion according to ascending powers of \(q\). By (2), the expansions of the \(v\)'s have the beginnings
\[
 v_\infty=5q^{2/3}+\cdots,\qquad
 v_z=e^{\frac{c}{12}{2\pi i\over5}}q^{-2/15}+\cdots,
\]
and from this, using the known equation
\[
 4\sin {2\pi\over5}\sin {4\pi\over5}=\sqrt5,
\]
there follows the beginning of the expansion for \(w_z\):
\begin{equation}
 w_z=q^{-2/5}e^{c\pi i/10}+\cdots.
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
It follows from this that the power sums of the \(w_z\), up to and including the fourth, are constants, since they cannot contain even the first power of \(j(\omega)\), and that the product of the \(w_z\) must be a linear function of \(j(\omega)\) in which \(j(\omega)\) has coefficient \(1\).

The equation of the \(w\)'s therefore has the form
\begin{equation}
 w^5+b_1w^4+b_2w^3+b_3w^2+b_4w+b_5=j(\omega),
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
where the \(b\)'s are rational numbers. These coefficients can be calculated by continuing the expansion (4). We take here another path, which, strictly speaking, belongs to the complex multiplication treated in the next part, but which, because of its simplicity, can nevertheless very well find its place here.

Put \(\omega=i=\sqrt{-1}\). Then
\[
 \omega=-{1\over\omega},
\]
and consequently [\S~34, (11), (12), (14)]
\[
 f_1(i)=f_2(i)=\sqrt[24]{2},\qquad f(i)=\sqrt[12]{2},
\]
hence [\S~54, (5)]
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_3(i)=0,\qquad \gamma_2(i)=12,\qquad j(i)=12^3.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
Furthermore one finds from the transformation formulae for \(\eta(\omega)\) [\S~34, (4), (5)]
\[
 \eta(\omega+1)=e^{\pi i/12}\eta(\omega),
 \qquad
 \eta\!\left(-{1\over\omega}\right)=\sqrt{-i\omega}\,\eta(\omega),
\]
if the decomposition
\[
5=(2+i)(2-i)
\]
is taken into account:
\[
 \eta\!\left({12+i\over5}\right)
 =e^{\pi i/6}\eta\!\left({2+i\over5}\right)
 =e^{\pi i/6}\eta\!\left({1\over2-i}\right)
 =\sqrt{1+2i}\,\eta(i),
\]
therefore
\[
 v_2=1+2i,\qquad v_3=1-2i.
\]
Now, since for \(\gamma_2=12\) two roots of equation (1) are known, for this special value of \(\gamma_2\) the left hand side of this equation can easily be decomposed into factors:
\[
 v^6+10v^3-12v+5=(v^2+2v+5)(v^2+v-1)^2,
\]
from which one concludes
\begin{equation}
 v_\infty=v_0={-1+\sqrt5\over2},\qquad
 v_1=v_4={-1-\sqrt5\over2}.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
(The quantities \(v_0,v_\infty\) must be positive, since \(q\) is real in them.)

From (3) one then obtains
\begin{equation}
 w_0=0,\qquad
 w_1=w_3=-5-2i\sqrt5,\qquad
 w_2=w_4=-5+2i\sqrt5.
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
These must be the roots of (5) for \(j=12^3\), and from this one obtains the desired resolvent in the form
\begin{equation}
 w(w^2+10w+45)^2=j(\omega)-12^3=\gamma_3^2.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
This equation can also be brought into the form
\begin{equation}
 (w+3)^3(w^2+11w+64)=j(\omega)=\gamma_2^3,
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
to which one also comes directly if, instead of putting \(\omega=i\), one assumes
\[
 \omega={-1+i\sqrt3\over2}.
\]

The resolvent found is simplified further if, according to (9), one puts
\[
 z=\sqrt w={\gamma_3\over w^2+10w+45}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 z^5+10z^3+45z=\gamma_3.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
Or, if according to (10), one puts
\begin{equation}
 w^2+11w+64=y^3,\qquad y={\gamma_2\over w+3},
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 y^5-40y^2-5\gamma_2y-\gamma_2^2=0.
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
This equation also results if one makes from the outset the assumption
\[
 2y=v_\infty v_0+v_1v_4+v_2v_3.
\]

In equation (13), as one sees, the third and fourth powers of the unknown are absent. The same property belongs also, as is easy to prove, to the equation whose roots are
\begin{equation}
 x=y(\lambda+\mu z)={(\lambda+\mu z)\gamma_2\over z^2+3},
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda,\mu\) denote arbitrary parameters which are the same for all five values of \(x\).

If this equation is put in the form
\begin{equation}
 x^5-5ax^3-5b\gamma_2 x-bc\gamma_2^2=0,
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
then, after some calculations using the special values of \(x\) for \(\gamma_3=0\) and \(\gamma_3=\infty\), one obtains for \(a,b,c\) the following expressions:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 a&=8\lambda^3-72\lambda\mu^2+\gamma_3(\lambda^2\mu-\mu^3),\\
 b&=\lambda^4+18\lambda^2\mu^2-27\mu^4+\gamma_3\lambda\mu^3,\\
 c&=\lambda^5+10\lambda^3\mu^2+45\lambda\mu^3+\gamma_3\mu^5.
\end{aligned}
\tag{16}
\end{equation}

One more resolvent of the fifth degree is to be formed by using the function \(f(\omega)\).

We put
\begin{equation}
 u=f(\omega),\qquad v_\infty=f(5\omega),\qquad
 v_z=f\!\left({\omega+c\over5}\right),
\quad c\equiv0\pmod{48},\quad z\equiv c\pmod5,
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
and, according to \S~73, have between \(u\) and \(v\) the equation of the sixth degree
\begin{equation}
 u^6+v^6-u^5v^5+4uv=0.
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
We investigate the influence which the three interchanges
\[
(\omega,\omega+2),\qquad
\left(\omega,-{1\over\omega}\right),\qquad
\left(\omega,{\omega-1\over\omega+1}\right)
\]
have on the quantities \(v_z\). Under these interchanges \(c\) passes into \(c_1,c_2,c'\), which, by \S~69, (9), (11) and \S~73, (6), are determined by the congruences
\begin{equation}
 c_1\equiv c+2,\qquad c_2\equiv-{1\over c},\qquad
 c'\equiv {c-1\over c+1}\pmod5.
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
Apart from this change of the index, by \S~73, (4) and (9), the \(v\)'s pass into
\begin{equation}
 e^{-5\pi i/12}v,\qquad v,\qquad -{\sqrt2\over v}.
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
Thus, if for abbreviation one puts \(e^{-5\pi i/12}=\varepsilon\), one has the following corresponding interchanges:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|ccccccc}
&u&v_\infty&v_0&v_1&v_2&v_3&v_4\\ \hline
\omega+2&
 e^{-\pi i/12}u&\varepsilon v_\infty&\varepsilon v_2&\varepsilon v_3&\varepsilon v_4&\varepsilon v_0&\varepsilon v_1\\
-1/\omega&
 u&v_0&v_\infty&v_4&v_2&v_3&v_1\\
(\omega-1)/(\omega+1)&
 {\sqrt2\over u}&-{\sqrt2\over v_1}&-{\sqrt2\over v_4}&-{\sqrt2\over v_0}
 &-{\sqrt2\over v_2}&-{\sqrt2\over v_3}&-{\sqrt2\over v_\infty}
\end{array}
\tag{21}
\end{equation}

We now introduce the five quantities \(w_z\) by the equation
\begin{equation}
 w_z={(v_\infty-v_z)(v_{z+1}-v_{z-1})(v_{z+2}-v_{z-2})\over \sqrt5\,u^3},
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
that is,
\[
\begin{aligned}
w_0&={(v_\infty-v_0)(v_1-v_4)(v_2-v_3)\over\sqrt5\,u^3},\\
w_1&={(v_\infty-v_1)(v_2-v_0)(v_3-v_4)\over\sqrt5\,u^3},\\
w_2&={(v_\infty-v_2)(v_3-v_1)(v_4-v_0)\over\sqrt5\,u^3},\\
w_3&={(v_\infty-v_3)(v_4-v_2)(v_0-v_1)\over\sqrt5\,u^3},\\
w_4&={(v_\infty-v_4)(v_0-v_3)(v_1-v_2)\over\sqrt5\,u^3}.
\end{aligned}
\]
If in the last row one uses the fact that by (18) the product of the six quantities \(v\) has the value \(u^6\), then from (21) there result the following corresponding interchanges of the \(w\)'s:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|ccccc}
& w_0&w_1&w_2&w_3&w_4\\ \hline
\omega+2&-w_2&-w_3&-w_4&-w_0&-w_1\\
-1/\omega&w_0&w_2&w_1&w_4&w_3\\
(\omega-1)/(\omega+1)&-w_0&-w_3&-w_4&-w_2&-w_1
\end{array}
\tag{23}
\end{equation}

The functions \(w\) can become infinite for no value of \(u\) different from \(0\) and \(\infty\). If therefore we assume the equation whose roots are the five quantities \(w_z\) in the form
\[
 w^5+A_1w^4+A_2w^3+A_3w^2+A_4w+A_5=0,
\]
then, by the principles of \S~73, \(A_1^2,A_2,A_3^2,A_4,A_5^2\) are integral rational functions of
\begin{equation}
 u^{24}+{2^{12}\over u^{24}}.
\tag{24}
\end{equation}
The expansion of (24) according to ascending powers of \(q\) begins with \(q^{-1}\), whereas the beginning of the expansion of \(w_z\) is
\[
 q^{-1/10}e^{-c\pi i/60}.
\]
The quantities \(A_1^2,A_2,A_3^2,A_4\) therefore cannot contain even the first power of (24), and so are constants, while \(A_5^2\) has the form
\[
 A_5^2=u^{24}+{2^{12}\over u^{24}}+C,
\]
where \(C\) is a constant. The values of the constants can be determined if one knows the values of the \(w_z\) for \(\omega=i\).

But for \(\omega=i\) [compare above (6), (7)] one has
\[
 u=f(i)=\sqrt[24]{2},
\]
\[
 v_3=f\!\left({i+48\over5}\right)
 =f\!\left({i-2\over5}+10\right)
 =e^{-10\pi i/24}f\!\left({i-2\over5}\right)
 =e^{-10\pi i/24}f(i+2)=-i\sqrt[24]{2},
\]
\[
 v_2=i\sqrt[24]{2},
\]
and consequently, by (18),
\[
 v_\infty=v_0=\sqrt[24]{2}{1+\sqrt5\over2},\qquad
 v_1=v_4=\sqrt[24]{2}{1-\sqrt5\over2}.
\]
Consequently
\[
 w_0=0,\qquad w_1=w_2=i\sqrt5,\qquad w_3=w_4=-i\sqrt5.
\]
First this gives \(C=-2^7\), hence
\[
 A_5^2=\left(u^{12}-{64\over u^{12}}\right)^2,
\]
and from this, by comparing the beginnings of the expansion,
\[
 A_5=-u^{12}+{64\over u^{12}},
\]
and the equation for \(w\) receives the form
\begin{equation}
 w(w^2+5)^2=u^{12}-{64\over u^{12}}.
\tag{25}
\end{equation}

But one can also give it another, very remarkable form.

By the relations existing between the functions \(f(\omega),f_1(\omega),f_2(\omega)\) [\S~34, (11), (12)], one has
\[
 {f(\omega)^{24}-64\over f(\omega)^{12}}
 ={[f_1(\omega)^8-f_2(\omega)^8]^2\over f(\omega)^4}.
\]
Thus, if for \(w\) we introduce the new unknown
\begin{equation}
 y=\sqrt w={f_1(\omega)^8-f_2(\omega)^8\over f(\omega)^2(w^2+5)},
\tag{26}
\end{equation}
we obtain the form
\begin{equation}
 y^5+5y={f_1(\omega)^8-f_2(\omega)^8\over f(\omega)^2}.
\tag{27}
\end{equation}

On these formulae rests the solution, created by Hermite and Kronecker, of the equation of the fifth degree by elliptic functions, to which we referred in the fourteenth section of Volume II.

According to Volume I, \S~60, the general equation of the fifth degree can be reduced to the form
\begin{equation}
 z^5+5z=a,
\tag{28}
\end{equation}
and this equation becomes identical with (27) if one puts
\[
 f_1(\omega)^8-f_2(\omega)^8=af(\omega)^2.
\]
Adding the equations
\[
 f_1^8+f_2^8=f^8,\qquad ff_1f_2=\sqrt2,
\]
one obtains
\begin{equation}
 f^{24}-a^2f^{12}-64=0.
\tag{29}
\end{equation}
By this quadratic equation, \(f^{12}\) is determined as a function of \(a\).

Then the roots of equation (28) are represented by the square roots of the expressions (22). The double sign of these square roots is explained by the fact that two opposite values of \(a\) lead to the same equation (29). But the roots can also be determined unambiguously by formula (26):
\begin{equation}
 z={a\over w^2+5}.
\tag{30}
\end{equation}

In the above-mentioned work of Kiepert, the general equation of the fifth degree is transformed into the form (15), for which only the solution of a quadratic equation is required. If in it \(a,b,c\) are regarded as arbitrarily given, then equations (16) serve for the determination of \(\lambda,\mu,\gamma_3\). This too occurs with the help of a quadratic equation, although this is not apparent at first glance. (Compare F. Klein, \emph{Vorlesungen über das Ikosaeder}, p.~191 f.)

We shall return below to the formation of resolvents for the seventh transformation degree, when we have at our disposal the aids supplied by complex multiplication.


\section*{Volume III. Second Book. Quadratic Fields}
\section*{Ninth Section. Discriminant}
\section*{\S\,84. Definition of discriminants}

In the theory of quadratic fields there occur, as discriminants, certain rational integers, positive or negative, which, if they are even, are divisible by \(4\), and, if they are odd, leave the remainder \(1\) when divided by \(4\).  Kronecker called such numbers ``numbers of discriminant form.''  We shall here call them simply discriminants, without being disturbed by the fact that the word has many other meanings in algebra.  We define:

1. A positive or negative non-zero integer which satisfies one of the two congruences
\begin{equation}
        D\equiv0,\quad D\equiv1\pmod 4
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
is called a discriminant.

Every square number is, by this definition, a discriminant.  Since, however, squares occupy a certain exceptional position, we shall call them improper discriminants when a distinction is necessary.

2. The product of two, and consequently of any number of, discriminants is again a discriminant.

3. A discriminant which, apart from \(1\), contains no quadratic divisor after whose removal a discriminant remains is called a fundamental discriminant.  Fundamental discriminants will subsequently be denoted by \(\Delta\).  If \(D\) is not a fundamental discriminant, there is one and only one square \(Q^2\) and one fundamental discriminant \(\Delta\) such that
\begin{equation}
        D=Q^2\Delta.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
Then \(\Delta\) is called the fundamental discriminant, or the stem, of \(D\).

A fundamental discriminant is divisible by no odd square.  If, however, \(\Delta\) is divisible by \(4\), then necessarily
\begin{equation}
        \Delta\equiv8,12\pmod {16}.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
For if \(\Delta\equiv0,4\pmod {16}\), then after removing the factor \(4\) a discriminant would still remain.  Thus, in order to find the stem of a given \(D\), one first removes the greatest odd square divisor and then a sufficiently high power of \(4\), until the remaining \(\Delta\) is either odd and \(\equiv1\pmod4\), or is \(\equiv8\) or \(12\pmod {16}\).

4. A proper discriminant which is not decomposable into factors which themselves are discriminants is called a prime discriminant.  If \(p\) is an odd natural prime number, and the sign is so chosen that \(\pm p\equiv1\pmod4\), then the prime discriminants are
\begin{equation}
        \pm p,\\ -4,\\ +8,\\ -8.
\tag{4}
\end{equation}

5. Every fundamental discriminant can be decomposed in one and only one way into prime discriminants.  For if one separates from \(\Delta\) first all factors \(\pm p\), only one of the numbers \(1,-4,+8,-8\) remains.

\section*{\S\,85. The extended Legendre-Jacobi symbol}

The Legendre symbol \(\Jac{m}{p}\), when \(m\) is any positive or negative non-zero integer and \(p\) is a natural prime number not dividing \(m\), has the value \(+1\) if \(m\) is a quadratic residue of \(p\), and the value \(-1\) if \(m\) is a quadratic non-residue of \(p\).  Jacobi extended the meaning of the symbol so that a composite number can stand in place of \(p\).  If \(m\) is a discriminant, however, a still further extension is often convenient.

If \(D\) is a discriminant and \(n\) an integer, we define a symbol \((D,n)\) by the following rules:
\begin{align}
        (D,0)&=0,\tag{1}\\
        (D,1)&=1,\tag{2}\\
        (D,-1)&=\begin{cases}1,&D>0,\\ -1,&D<0,\end{cases}\tag{3}\\
        (D,p)&=0\quad\hbox{if }p\mid D,\tag{4}\\
        (D,2)&=(-1)^{(D^2-1)/8},\quad D\hbox{ odd},\tag{5}\\
        (D,p)&=\Jac{D}{p}\quad(p\hbox{ odd prime},\ p\nmid D).\tag{6}
\end{align}
If \(\pm n\) is decomposed into prime factors,
\[
        \pm n=p_1p_2\cdots p_r,
\]
then
\begin{equation}
        (D,n)=(D,\pm1)(D,p_1)(D,p_2)\cdots(D,p_r).
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
The symbol is thereby defined without contradiction for all \(n\), and has only the values \(-1,0,+1\).  It is zero precisely when \(D\) and \(n\) are not relatively prime.  From (7) follows
\begin{equation}
        (D,nn')=(D,n)(D,n'),
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
and, if \(q^2\) is a square prime to \(n\),
\begin{equation}
        (q^2D,n)=(D,n).
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

The formulas belonging to the law of reciprocity take a special form in this notation.  If \(\pm p\equiv1\pmod4\), then for every \(n\) not divisible by \(p\),
\begin{equation}
        (\pm p,n)=\Jac{\pm p}{n}.
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
For odd \(n\) one has likewise
\begin{equation}
        (-4,n)=(-1)^{(n-1)/2},\qquad
        (8,n)=(-1)^{(n^2-1)/8},\qquad
        (-8,n)=(-1)^{(n-1)/2+(n^2-1)/8}.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
Further, if \(D,D'\) are two discriminants, then
\begin{equation}
        (DD',n)=(D,n)(D',n).
\tag{12}
\end{equation}

The reciprocity law with its supplementary theorems can then be summarized as
\begin{equation}
        (D,D')=\pm(D',D),
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
where the upper sign holds when at least one of the two discriminants \(D,D'\) is positive, and the lower sign when both are negative.

Since every square is a positive discriminant, one obtains, if \(m\) is prime to \(D\),
\begin{equation}
        (D,m^2)=1,
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
and, together with (8),
\begin{equation}
        (D,mn^2)=(D,m)
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
whenever \(mn\) and \(D\) have no common divisor.  Moreover
\begin{equation}
        (D,n)=(D,n')\quad\hbox{if }n\equiv n'\pmod D.
\tag{16}
\end{equation}

If \(n,n'\) in (16) are themselves discriminants, one obtains from (13) and (16)
\begin{equation}
        (D_1,D)=\pm(D_2,D),
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
where the lower sign occurs only when the two numbers \(D\) and \(D_1D_2\) are both negative.  If \(D_1,D_2\) are odd and \(D=4m\), this gives
\begin{equation}
        (D_1,m)=\pm(D_2,m),
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
provided
\begin{equation}
        D_1\equiv D_2\pmod {4m}.
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
The same formula holds in general.  The formulas (16) and (18) can be used to compute the symbol \((D,n)\) by the algorithm of the greatest common divisor.

If \(D\) is not a square, one can always determine a number \(\beta\) such that
\begin{equation}
        (D,\beta)=-1.
\tag{21}
\end{equation}
For if \(\Delta\) is the stem of \(D\), and \(\beta\) is prime to \(D\), then \((D,\beta)=(\Delta,\beta)\).  Decomposing \(\Delta\) into prime discriminants, one chooses \(\beta\) to make one factor give \(-1\) and the others give \(+1\).  Therefore, if \(s\) runs through a complete system of residues modulo \(D\), and if
\[
        S=\sum_s (D,s),
\]
then multiplication by such a \(\beta\) gives also \(S=-S\), and therefore
\begin{equation}
        S=0.
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
Thus in a complete system of incongruent numbers prime to \(D\) there are as many numbers \(\alpha\) with \((D,\alpha)=+1\) as numbers \(\beta\) with \((D,\beta)=-1\).

\section*{\S\,86. The Gaussian sums}

In the first volume we became acquainted with the Gaussian sums from the theory of cyclotomy.  These expressions can be generalized, and, by using the symbol \((D,n)\), take a simple form.

It was shown previously that, if \(n\) is an odd prime number, if \(s\) is not divisible by \(n\), and if \(k\) runs through a complete residue system modulo \(n\), then
\begin{equation}
        \sum_k \Jac{k}{n}e^{2\pi isk/n}=\Jac{s}{n}\,\sqrt{\pm n},
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
with the usual choice of sign according as \(n\equiv1\) or \(3\pmod4\).  Put \(\Delta=\pm n\equiv1\pmod4\).  Then \(\Delta\) is a prime discriminant, and, by \S 85,
\[
        (\Delta,k)=\Jac{\Delta}{k}=\Jac{k}{n},
\]
with the corresponding supplementary signs included.  Formula (1) may therefore be written
\begin{equation}
        \sum_k (\Delta,k)e^{2\pi isk/\Delta}=(\Delta,s)\sqrt{\Delta},
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where \(\sqrt{\Delta}\) is positive real or positive imaginary according as \(\Delta\) is positive or negative.  Direct calculation shows that the same formula also holds for
\[
        \Delta=-4,\quad +8,\quad -8,
\]
and hence for every prime discriminant.

Assume the formula true for two relatively prime fundamental discriminants \(\Delta'
\) and \(\Delta''\).  Since
\begin{equation}
        \sqrt{\Delta'}\sqrt{\Delta''}=\pm\sqrt{\Delta'\Delta''},
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
where the lower sign occurs only when both factors are negative, put
\[
        k=k'\Delta''-k''\Delta'.
\]
As \(k',k''\) run through complete residue systems modulo \(\Delta',\Delta''\), the number \(k\) runs through a complete residue system modulo \(\Delta=\Delta'\Delta''\).  Multiplication of the two sums gives
\begin{equation}
        \sum_k(\Delta,k)e^{2\pi isk/\Delta}.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
Using \S 85, (16) and the reciprocity formula \S 85, (13), one obtains
\[
        (\Delta,k)=(\Delta',k')(
\Delta'',k''),
\]
and consequently (2) is valid for the product \(\Delta'\Delta''\).  By decomposition into prime discriminants, formula (2) is therefore valid for every fundamental discriminant \(\Delta\).


\section*{Tenth Section. Algebraic numbers and forms.}

\section*{§ 87. Ideals and forms in algebraic fields.}

The interest which the theory of elliptic functions has for the algebraist arises from its application to the theory of quadratic irrational numbers, to which it stands in an analogous relation as the roots of unity stand to the rational numbers. It offers us the first, and until now the only, example, more exactly known in its laws, of a domain of algebraic numbers which goes beyond the cyclotomic numbers. In order to present the theory of these numbers more closely, we must first place before it some theorems from the theory of forms and algebraic fields, and especially of quadratic forms.

In Vol. II, §§ 163, 169, a connection was obtained between the ideals of an algebraic field $\Omega$ of degree $n$ and certain homogeneous functions of degree $n$ in $n$ variables. Before applying this to quadratic fields, let us briefly recall the general theorems.

Let $\mathfrak a$ be an ideal of a field $\Omega$, and let $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n$ be a basis of this ideal. Further let $\omega_1,\omega_2,
\ldots,\omega_n$ be a minimal basis of $\Omega$. Then
\begin{equation}
 (\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n)=(A)(\omega_1,\omega_2,
\ldots,\omega_n),\tag{1}
\end{equation}
where $(A)$ denotes a linear substitution with integral coefficients. To pass to another basis $\alpha'_1,\alpha'_2,
\ldots,\alpha'_n$ of $\mathfrak a$, make a linear substitution $L$ with determinant $\pm1$,
\begin{equation}
 (\alpha'_1,\alpha'_2,
\ldots,\alpha'_n)=(L)(\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n),\tag{2}
\end{equation}
whence
\begin{equation}
 (\alpha'_1,\alpha'_2,
\ldots,\alpha'_n)=(L)(A)(\omega_1,\omega_2,
\ldots,\omega_n).\tag{3}
\end{equation}
By the norm $N(\mathfrak a)$ of the ideal $\mathfrak a$ one understands the absolute value of the determinant $A$ of the substitution $(A)$:
\[
 A=\sum\pm a_{1,1}a_{2,2}\cdots a_{n,n},
\]
and by (3) the norm is independent of the choice of basis.

1. We shall call the basis $(\alpha_\nu)$ positive if the determinant $A$ is positive, and therefore equal to the norm of $\mathfrak a$. If $(\alpha_\nu)$ is positive, then $(\alpha'_\nu)$ is positive precisely when the determinant of the substitution is $L=+1$.

From every basis one can derive a positive basis by a substitution with determinant $-1$, for example by interchanging two of the $\alpha_\nu$, and in what follows we shall mostly use only positive bases.

The basis determined in Vol. II, § 163, (3), is positive. Let $t_1,t_2,
\ldots,t_n$ be a system of independent variables and
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=\sum_r\alpha_rt_r\tag{4}
\end{equation}
a basis form with positive basis. Then (Vol. II, § 164)
\begin{equation}
 N(\lambda)=N(\mathfrak a)T,\tag{5}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 T=\sum\pm t_{1,1}t_{2,2}\cdots t_{n,n}\tag{6}
\end{equation}
is a primitive integral homogeneous form of degree $n$ in the variables $t_\nu$. The $t_{r,s}$ are linear functions of the $t_\nu$.

Applying the substitution (2) to (4), one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=\lambda'=\sum_r\alpha'_rt'_r,\tag{7}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 (t_1,t_2,\ldots,t_n)=L'(t'_1,t'_2,
\ldots,t'_n),\tag{8}
\end{equation}
and $L'$ is the transposed substitution of $L$. If $(\alpha'_\nu)$ is likewise a positive basis, then $L'$ has determinant $+1$, and formula (5) gives
\begin{equation}
 N(\lambda)=N(\mathfrak a)T',\tag{9}
\end{equation}
where $T'$ is a homogeneous function of degree $n$ in the variables $t'$ which arises from $T$ by the substitution (8).

Two forms which arise from one another by an integral linear substitution with determinant $+1$ are called equivalent. Sometimes the forms are also called equivalent when the determinant of the substitution is $-1$, but then with the qualification ``improperly.'' Thus (8) proves:

2. If we call $T$ the form belonging to the basis $(\alpha_\nu)$ of $\mathfrak a$, then the forms belonging to the different positive bases of the same ideal are equivalent.

Denote the conjugate values of $\alpha_s,\omega_s$ by
\[
 \alpha_{s,1},\alpha_{s,2},\ldots,\alpha_{s,n};\qquad
 \omega_{s,1},\omega_{s,2},\ldots,\omega_{s,n},
\]
and put
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_{s,r}=\sum_\nu a_{s,\nu}\omega_{\nu,r}.\tag{10}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \left(\sum\pm\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}\cdots\alpha_{n,n}\right)^2=N(\mathfrak a)^2\Delta,\tag{11}
\end{equation}
where $\Delta$ is the fundamental number of the field $\Omega$, namely the square of the determinant
\[
 \Delta=\left(\sum\pm\omega_{1,1}\omega_{2,2}\cdots\omega_{n,n}\right)^2
\]
(Vol. II, § 162).

If $\lambda_1,\lambda_2,
\ldots,\lambda_n$ are the conjugate values of $\lambda$, then by (4)
\begin{equation}
 \lambda_r=\sum_\nu\alpha_{\nu,r}t_\nu\tag{12}
\end{equation}
is a linear substitution for the variables $t_\nu$ with substitution determinant
\begin{equation}
 r=N(\mathfrak a)\sqrt{\Delta},\tag{13}
\end{equation}
and through this, by (5), the form $T$ passes into the product
\begin{equation}
 T'=[N(\mathfrak a)]^{-1}\lambda_1\lambda_2\cdots\lambda_n,\tag{14}
\end{equation}
because $N(\lambda)$ is the product of the conjugate values of $\lambda$ (Vol. II, § 151). We can apply invariant theory to this (Vol. I, § 65), and if we form the Hessian determinant
\begin{equation}
 H=\det\left(\frac{\partial^2T}{\partial t_i\partial t_j}\right),\tag{15}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 H'=r^{n-2}H.\tag{16}
\end{equation}
If, however, we form $H'$ from (14) in the variables $\lambda_\nu$ and observe that an $n$-rowed determinant whose diagonal terms are $0$ and all other terms are $1$ has the value $(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)$,
\footnote{One can easily prove this theorem by adjoining an $(n+1)$st row and an $(n+1)$st column to the determinant, in such a way that in the adjoined row only ones occur and in the column, except for the last element, zeros occur.}
then
\[
 H'=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)N(\mathfrak a)^{-n}(\lambda_1\lambda_2\cdots\lambda_n)^{n-2}
 =(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)N(\mathfrak a)^{-2}T^{n-2},
\]
and consequently, by (13) and (16),
\begin{equation}
 H=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)T^{n-2}\Delta.\tag{17}
\end{equation}

\section*{§ 88. Ideal classes and form classes.}

In Vol. II, § 170, we explained the equivalence of ideals and the ideal classes. Accordingly two ideals $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak b$ were equivalent if there is an integral or fractional number of the field $\Omega$ which is the quotient of $\mathfrak b$ and $\mathfrak a$, that is,
\begin{equation}
 \eta\mathfrak a=\mathfrak b,\tag{1}
\end{equation}
and it was shown that the ideals are thereby divided into classes, and that the number of these classes is finite. In some cases it is expedient to take the concept of equivalence somewhat more narrowly and to call $\mathfrak a$ and $\mathfrak b$ equivalent only when there is a number $\eta$ satisfying condition (1) with positive norm. This distinction naturally does not arise when the norms of all numbers are positive, as for instance in the imaginary quadratic field. It also does not arise when there are units of norm $-1$, since, if $\varepsilon$ is such a unit, then either $N(\eta)$ or $N(\varepsilon\eta)$ is positive, and $\varepsilon$ and $\varepsilon\eta$ simultaneously satisfy condition (1).

But if there are no units whose norm is $-1$, although there are other numbers in $\Omega$ with negative norm, then under the narrower definition of equivalence each ideal class divides once more into two classes. We shall keep here to the narrower concept of equivalence, which for example is in force in some real quadratic fields.
\footnote{Namely, when Pell's equation $t^2-Du^2=-4$ has no solution.}

In (1), $\eta$ may be fractional. But if $\alpha$ is an integral number divisible by $\mathfrak a$, then $\eta\alpha$ is an integral number divisible by $\mathfrak b$, and from this one obtains the following. If
\begin{equation}
 (\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n)\tag{2}
\end{equation}
is a basis of $\mathfrak a$, then
\[
 (\beta_1,\beta_2,
\ldots,\beta_n)=(\alpha_1\eta,\alpha_2\eta,
\ldots,\alpha_n\eta)
\]
is a basis of $\mathfrak b$; and if the first basis is positive, then, under the narrower concept of equivalence, so is the second.

For the necessary and sufficient condition that (2) be a basis of $\mathfrak a$ is that every integral number $\alpha$ divisible by $\mathfrak a$, and only such a number, be contained in the form
\begin{equation}
 \alpha=x_1\alpha_1+x_2\alpha_2+\cdots+x_n\alpha_n\tag{3}
\end{equation}
with integral $x_1,x_2,
\ldots,x_n$. Consequently all numbers
\begin{equation}
 \beta=x_1\alpha_1\eta+x_2\alpha_2\eta+\cdots+x_n\alpha_n\eta\tag{4}
\end{equation}
are divisible by $\mathfrak b$. Conversely, if $\beta$ is an integral number divisible by $\mathfrak b$, then $\beta/\eta$ is divisible by $\mathfrak a$ and hence representable in the form (3). Thus $\beta$ is representable by (4).

Furthermore, using the notation of (9) and (10), § 87, when $\alpha$ is a positive basis:
\[
 \sum\pm \alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}\cdots\alpha_{n,n}=N(\mathfrak a)\sum\pm \omega_{1,1}\omega_{2,2}\cdots\omega_{n,n},
\]
\[
 \sum\pm \beta_{1,1}\beta_{2,2}\cdots\beta_{n,n}=N(\eta)
 \sum\pm \alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}\cdots\alpha_{n,n}
 =\pm N(\mathfrak b)
 \sum\pm \omega_{1,1}\omega_{2,2}\cdots\omega_{n,n},
\]
and since by (1)
\begin{equation}
 N(\mathfrak b)=N(\eta)N(\mathfrak a),\tag{5}
\end{equation}
the positive sign must stand at $\pm N(\mathfrak b)$.

If $\lambda$ is a basis form of $\mathfrak a$, then $\eta\lambda$ is a basis form of $\mathfrak b$, and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 N(\lambda)&=N(\mathfrak a)T,\\
 N(\eta\lambda)&=N(\mathfrak b)T,
 \end{aligned}\tag{6}
\end{equation}
and hence the same form $T$ belongs to the two ideals $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak b$. If equivalent forms $T$ are combined into a class, we can say by 2.:

3. To every ideal class there belongs a determinate form class.

In answering the question whether different ideal classes can belong to one and the same form class, we make the assumption that $\Omega$ is a normal field. Suppose, then, that equivalent forms $T$ and $T'$ belong to two ideals $\mathfrak a$ and $\mathfrak a'$. If $\lambda$ is a basis form of $\mathfrak a$, then
\begin{equation}
 N(\lambda)=N(\mathfrak a)T,\tag{7}
\end{equation}
and if we carry $T'$ into $T$ by a linear substitution, and understand by $\lambda'$ a basis form of $\mathfrak a'$ (with the same variables $t$ as $\lambda$), then
\begin{equation}
 N(\lambda')=N(\mathfrak a')T.\tag{8}
\end{equation}
The linear factors of $N(\lambda)$ and $N(\lambda')$ must therefore agree with one another, apart from a constant factor, since an integral function of arbitrary variables can be decomposed into irreducible factors (here linear factors) in only one way (Vol. I, § 20, Vol. II, § 152); and since all these factors belong to the same field $\Omega$, there is among the factors conjugate to $\lambda'$ one which satisfies the condition
\[
 \lambda=\eta\lambda',
\]
where $\eta$ is a number in $\Omega$. The functionals $\lambda$ and $\lambda'$, and therefore the corresponding ideals, are thus equivalent.

4. If, in a normal field, the same form class belongs to two ideals $\mathfrak a$ and $\mathfrak a'$, then among the ideals conjugate to $\mathfrak a'$ there is one which is equivalent to $\mathfrak a$.

\section*{§ 89. Composition of forms and multiplication of ideals.}

Let $\mathfrak a$ and $\mathfrak b$ now be two ideals in $\Omega$ and let
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak c=\mathfrak a\mathfrak b\tag{1}
\end{equation}
be the product formed from them. Further let
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathfrak a&=(\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n),\\
 \mathfrak b&=(\beta_1,\beta_2,
\ldots,\beta_n),\\
 \mathfrak c&=(\gamma_1,\gamma_2,
\ldots,\gamma_n)
\end{aligned}\tag{2}
\end{equation}
be bases of these ideals. Since each number $\alpha_h\beta_k$ belongs to $\mathfrak c$, there is a system of integral rational numbers $c_{h,k}^{(r)}$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_h\beta_k=\sum_r c_{h,k}^{(r)}\gamma_r,\tag{3}
\end{equation}
and since conversely each number in $\mathfrak c$ arises by multiplying one number from $\mathfrak a$ and one from $\mathfrak b$ and adding these products (Vol. II, § 169), one also has
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_s=\sum_{h,k}a_{h,k}^{(s)}\alpha_h\beta_k,\tag{4}
\end{equation}
where the $a_{h,k}^{(s)}$ are likewise integral rational numbers.

Substituting (3) in (4) gives
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_s=\sum_r\gamma_r\sum_{h,k}a_{h,k}^{(s)}c_{h,k}^{(r)}\tag{5}
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h,k}a_{h,k}^{(s)}c_{h,k}^{(r)}=(r,s),\tag{6}
\end{equation}
where $(r,r)=1$, $(r,s)=0$, $r\ne s$.

Thus let $x_r$ denote variables and put
\begin{equation}
 \sum_r x_rc_{h,k}^{(r)}=y_{h,k}.\tag{7}
\end{equation}
It follows that
\begin{equation}
 x_s=\sum_{h,k}a_{h,k}^{(s)}y_{h,k}.\tag{8}
\end{equation}
Now let $u_r,v_r,t_r$ denote three systems of variables and
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mu&=\sum\alpha_ru_r,\\
 \nu&=\sum\beta_rv_r,\\
 \lambda&=\sum\gamma_rt_r
\end{aligned}\tag{9}
\end{equation}
basis forms of $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak b,\mathfrak c$, and let $U,V,T$ be the forms belonging to $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak b,\mathfrak c$, written in the variables $u,v,t$. Then
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 N(\mu)&=N(\mathfrak a)U,\\
 N(\nu)&=N(\mathfrak b)V,\\
 N(\lambda)&=N(\mathfrak c)T.
\end{aligned}\tag{10}
\end{equation}
From (3), by multiplication with $u_hv_k$ and summation according to (9), one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \mu\nu=\sum_r\gamma_r\sum_{h,k}c_{h,k}^{(r)}u_hv_k,\tag{11}
\end{equation}
so that, if
\begin{equation}
 t_r=\sum_{h,k}c_{h,k}^{(r)}u_hv_k\tag{12}
\end{equation}
is put,
\begin{equation}
 \mu\nu=\lambda,\tag{13}
\end{equation}
and since $N(\mathfrak a)N(\mathfrak b)=N(\mathfrak c)$, it follows by (10) that
\begin{equation}
 T=UV.\tag{14}
\end{equation}
Here, however, the $t_r$ are no longer independent variables; rather, they arise from $u_h,v_k$ by the bilinear substitution (12).

5. The form $T$ passes, by the bilinear substitution (12), into the product of the two forms $U,V$. This substitution, however, has one more essential peculiarity. We call the functions $t_r$ determined by (12) linearly independent modulo a prime number $p$ if from the congruence
\begin{equation}
 \sum x_rt_r=0\pmod p,\tag{15}
\end{equation}
where the $x_r$ are integral rational numbers, it follows that all these numbers are divisible by $p$. If this is the case for every arbitrary prime number $p$, the $t_r$ are simply called linearly independent.

The congruence (15), by (12) and (7), is equivalent to the $n^2$ congruences
\[
 y_{h,k}=\sum_r x_rc_{h,k}^{(r)}\equiv0\pmod p,
\]
and then (6) gives
\[
 x_s\equiv0\pmod p.
\]
This says that the substitutions (12) are linearly independent for every modulus $p$.

According to Gauss (Disq. aritm. art. 235), a form $T$ is called composed or compounded from the two forms $U,V$ if $T$ passes into the product $UV$ by a bilinear substitution for the variables $t$ whose equations are linearly independent for every modulus.

Consequently we have the theorem:

6. The form which belongs to the product of two ideals $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak b$ is composed from the forms of the ideals $\mathfrak a$ and $\mathfrak b$.
\footnote{The theorems which Gauss proves at the cited place for binary quadratic forms by a very extensive calculation are here derived in the greatest generality by simple considerations. Cf. Dirichlet-Dedekind, Lectures on Number Theory, § 182. For binary quadratic forms: Dedekind, Crelle's Journal, vol. 129; H. Weber, Göttinger Nachrichten 1907.}




\section*{Eleventh Section. Ideals in quadratic fields.}

\section*{§ 90. Discriminant of the quadratic field.}

A quadratic field arises when one adjoins a square root $\sqrt d$ to the field of rational numbers; here $d$ may be assumed to be an integer without a square divisor. The field is real or imaginary according as $d$ is positive or negative. If one denotes the field of rational numbers, the absolute domain of rationality, by $\mathfrak R$, and by $\mathfrak R(x,x',\ldots)$ the field which arises by adjoining $x,x',\ldots$ to $\mathfrak R$, then the quadratic field $\Omega$ is to be denoted by
\begin{equation}
 \Omega=\mathfrak R(\sqrt d).
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
Every number of the field $\Omega$ can be put in the form
\begin{equation}
 \omega=\frac{x+y\sqrt d}{2},
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $x,y$ are rational integral or fractional numbers. The number conjugate to $\omega$,
\begin{equation}
 \omega'=\frac{x-y\sqrt d}{2},
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
is likewise contained in the field $\Omega$, and consequently $\Omega$ is a normal field.

In order that $\omega$ be an integer it is necessary that
\[
 \omega+\omega'=x,
 \qquad (\omega-\omega')^2=y^2d
\]
be rational integers; and since $d$ is to have no square divisor, $x$ and $y$ must be integers. But in order that $\omega$ really be an integer it is also still necessary that the norm $\frac14(x^2-y^2d)$ be a rational integer, that is, it must be
\begin{equation}
 x^2-y^2d\equiv0\pmod4 .
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
If $d\equiv2$ or $\equiv3\pmod4$, then this condition can be fulfilled only when $x$ and $y$ are even, and if therefore we replace $x,y$ by $2x,2y$, there follows:

\medskip
\noindent 1. If $d\equiv2,3\pmod4$, then all and only those numbers of the field $\Omega$ are integral which are contained in the form
\[
 x+y\sqrt d
\]
with rational integers $x,y$.
\medskip

If, however, $d\equiv1\pmod4$, then (3) is satisfied when $x$ and $y$ are both even or both odd, hence when $x$ has the form $2x_1-y$. If one then again replaces $x_1$ by $x$, one obtains:

\medskip
\noindent 2. If $d\equiv1\pmod4$, then all and only those numbers in $\Omega$ are integral which are contained in the form
\[
 x+y\frac{-1+\sqrt d}{2}
\]
with rational integers $x,y$.
\medskip

Thus in case 1, $(1,\sqrt d)$, and in case 2, $\left(1,\frac{-1+\sqrt d}{2}\right)$, is a minimal basis of the field $\Omega$. The fundamental number, or discriminant, of the field $\Omega$ is therefore, in case 1,
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=\begin{vmatrix}
 1&\sqrt d\\
 1&-\sqrt d
 \end{vmatrix}^{2}=4d,
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
and in case 2,
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=\begin{vmatrix}
 1&\dfrac{-1+\sqrt d}{2}\\[0.35em]
 1&\dfrac{-1-\sqrt d}{2}
 \end{vmatrix}^{2}=d.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
In both cases, therefore, the integral number $\omega$ can be put in the form
\begin{equation}
 \omega=\frac{x+y\sqrt\Delta}{2},
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
with the condition that
\begin{equation}
 4N(\omega)=x^2-\Delta y^2\equiv0\pmod4
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
be satisfied.

The two forms of the minimal basis which we have obtained, namely $(1,\sqrt d)$ and $\left(1,\frac{-1+\sqrt d}{2}\right)$, are therefore
\begin{equation}
 (1,\theta)=\left(1,\frac12\sqrt\Delta\right),\quad \Delta\equiv0,\qquad
 =\left(1,\frac{-1+\sqrt\Delta}{2}\right),\quad \Delta\equiv1\pmod4.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
Here the sign of $\sqrt\Delta$ may be determined in any arbitrary way, but in the same consideration it is then to be held fixed. We shall assume once for all:

\medskip
\noindent 3. If $\Delta$ is positive, then $\sqrt\Delta$ is likewise to be positive; and if $\Delta$ is negative, then $-i\sqrt\Delta$ is to be positive, or $\sqrt\Delta$ is to be positive imaginary.
\medskip

From (1), (2) there still follows:

\medskip
\noindent 4. The fundamental number of a quadratic field is always $\equiv0$ or $\equiv1$ modulo $4$ and, apart from $4$, has no square divisor. It is therefore a discriminant, indeed a fundamental discriminant.


\section*{\S\,91. Ideals and forms in quadratic fields.}

Let $\mathfrak a$ be an ideal of the quadratic field $\Omega$, let $\alpha_1,\alpha_2$ be a positive basis, and let
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=\alpha_1t_1+\alpha_2t_2
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
be a basis form of $\mathfrak a$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \Norm(\lambda)=(\alpha_1t_1+\alpha_2t_2)(\alpha_1't_1+\alpha_2't_2)=\Norm(\mathfrak a)T,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
and $\Norm(\mathfrak a)$ is the greatest common divisor of the three rational integers
\[
 \alpha_1\alpha_1',\qquad \alpha_1\alpha_2'+\alpha_2\alpha_1',\qquad \alpha_2\alpha_2'.
\]
Thus if we put
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\alpha_1\alpha_1'&=a\Norm(\mathfrak a),\\
\alpha_1\alpha_2'+\alpha_2\alpha_1'&=b\Norm(\mathfrak a),\\
\alpha_2\alpha_2'&=c\Norm(\mathfrak a),
\end{aligned}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
T=at_1^2+bt_1t_2+ct_2^2
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
is the form belonging to the basis $\alpha_1,\alpha_2$. We denote it, when the designation of the variables is immaterial, by
\begin{equation}
 \varphi=(a,b,c).
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
It is a primitive quadratic form whose discriminant
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=b^2-4ac
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
is equal to the fundamental number of the field $\Omega$ (\S\,87). To form the basis $\alpha_1,\alpha_2$ according to Vol. II, \S\,163, first seek the least natural number $a_{11}$ divisible by $\mathfrak a$, and then the least natural number $a_{22}$ for which a rational number $a_{12}$ satisfying the condition
\begin{equation}
 a_{12}+a_{22}\theta\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak a}
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
can be determined. Since the congruence (7) can be satisfied for $a_{22}=a_{11}$, namely by $a_{12}=0$, it follows that $a_{11}$ is divisible by $a_{22}$, and we put $a_{11}=a_{22}a$. Then we have the basis of $\mathfrak a$:
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_1=a_{22}a,
 \qquad
 \alpha_2=a_{12}+a_{22}\theta.
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
Then, according to Vol. II, \S\,164, (12),
\begin{equation}
 \Norm(\mathfrak a)=a_{22}^2a,
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
and if for $(1,\theta)$ we take the basis \S\,90, (9), then
\begin{equation}
\theta+\theta'=0\text{ or }=-1,
\qquad
\theta\theta'=-\frac14\Delta\text{ or }=\frac{1-\Delta}{4},
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
according as $\Delta\equiv0$ or $\equiv1\pmod4$, and
\[
 (\theta-\theta')^2=\Delta.
\]
In these two cases, if one substitutes (8) in (3) and takes account of (9), one obtains
\[
 a_{22}b=2a_{12}\quad\text{or}\quad =2a_{12}-a_{22},
\]
and hence in both cases
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_1=a_{22}a,
 \qquad
 \alpha_2=a_{22}\frac{b+\sqrt\Delta}{2};
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
and the form
\[
 T=(a,b,c)
\]
has here a positive first coefficient.

Put
\begin{equation}
 \omega=-\frac{\alpha_2}{\alpha_1}=-\frac{b+\sqrt\Delta}{2a}.
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
Then $\omega$ is a root of the quadratic equation
\begin{equation}
 a\omega^2+b\omega+c=0,
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=a_{22}a(x-\omega y)
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
is a functional corresponding to the ideal $\mathfrak a$ and at the same time a basis form of $\mathfrak a$. The number $a$ is the smallest positive integer for which $a\omega$ becomes an integer.

The necessary and sufficient condition that two integers $\alpha_1,\alpha_2$ of the field $\Omega$ form the basis of an ideal consists in this: first, $\alpha_1,\alpha_2$ must be a basis of the field $\Omega$; and, if $\omega_1,\omega_2$ is a minimal basis of this field, then
\[
 \alpha_1\omega_1,
 \quad \alpha_2\omega_1,
 \quad \alpha_1\omega_2,
 \quad \alpha_2\omega_2
\]
must be representable by the linear form
\[
 \lambda=\alpha_1t_1+\alpha_2t_2
\]
with integral $t_1,t_2$ (Vol. II, \S\,163, 164). For then, if $\alpha$ is representable by $\lambda$, every product $\omega\alpha$ is also representable by $\lambda$. In the present case this condition reduces to the requirement that $\alpha_1\theta$ and $\alpha_2\theta$ be representable by $\lambda$. But if $(a,b,c)$ is any quadratic form of discriminant $\Delta$ with positive first coefficient $a$, we can take for $\theta$ also $\frac12(b+\sqrt\Delta)$, and since
\[
 \left(\frac{b+\sqrt\Delta}{2}\right)^2
   =-ac+b\frac{b+\sqrt\Delta}{2},
\]
(11) is always the basis of an ideal in $\Omega$. Here the positive number $a_{22}$ may be assumed arbitrarily. If we put $a_{22}=1$, then
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=a(x-\omega y)
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
is the basis form of an ideal $\mathfrak a$ completely determined by $(a,b,c)$, whose norm is equal to $a$; and a basis of this ideal is
\begin{equation}
 (a,a\omega).
\tag{16}
\end{equation}

\section*{\S\,92. Prime ideals in the quadratic field.}

A prime number $p$ is, in the quadratic field $\Omega$, either itself still indecomposable, and then is a prime ideal of the second degree, or it decomposes into two prime ideals $\mathfrak p,\mathfrak p'$ of the first degree. Thus we have to distinguish two cases:
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
\text{a)}& p=\mathfrak p \text{ indecomposable in }\Omega: \quad \Norm(\mathfrak p)=p^2,\\[0.25em]
\text{b)}& p=\mathfrak p\mathfrak p',\qquad \Norm(\mathfrak p)=\Norm(\mathfrak p')=p.
\end{array}
\]
In the latter case the two prime ideals $\mathfrak p,\mathfrak p'$ can be distinct from one another or can also be equal, so that we must distinguish still a third case:
\[
\text{c)}\qquad p=\mathfrak p^2,\qquad \Norm(\mathfrak p)=p.
\]
The matter is now to determine the conditions under which one or another of these cases occurs.

If $\Norm(\mathfrak p)=p$, then $p$ is the number of incongruent numbers modulo $\mathfrak p$ in $\Omega$, and consequently, in this case, every number in $\Omega$ is congruent to one of the rational numbers
\begin{equation}
0,1,2,\ldots,p-1.
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
If, on the other hand, $\Norm(\mathfrak p)=p^2$, then there are also numbers which, modulo $\mathfrak p$, are not congruent to a rational number.

But if $(1,\theta)$ is a basis of $\Omega$ and, in order to include both cases of \S\,90, (9),
\begin{equation}
 \theta=\frac{-\Delta+\sqrt\Delta}{2}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
is assumed, then in case a) $\theta$ is not congruent to a number $r$, while in case b) or c)
\[
 \theta\equiv r\pmod{\mathfrak p}.
\]
If therefore one puts $-x=2r+\Delta$, it follows:

1. The necessary and sufficient condition for the occurrence of one of the cases b), c) is that there be a rational integer, even or odd simultaneously with $\Delta$, for which
\begin{equation}
 \frac{x+\sqrt\Delta}{2}\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p}.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
If $\mathfrak p'$ is the ideal conjugate to $\mathfrak p$, then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{-x+\sqrt\Delta}{2}\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p'},
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and from this follows
\begin{equation}
 x^2\equiv\Delta\pmod{4p}.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
Conversely, if the congruence (5) is satisfied by $x=r$, then
\[
 \frac{r+\sqrt\Delta}{2}\cdot\frac{-r+\sqrt\Delta}{2}\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p},
\]
and consequently either $x=r$ or $x=-r$ satisfies condition (3).

The two ideals $\mathfrak p,\mathfrak p'$ will then be identical when the two numbers (3) and (4), and consequently also their sum $\sqrt\Delta$, are divisible by $\mathfrak p$, and hence $\Delta$ is divisible by $p$. Then $x\equiv0$ or $x\equiv p\pmod{2p}$ is the only root of (5). We therefore have the result:

2. The prime factor $\mathfrak p$ of the natural prime number $p$ is of the second degree if the congruence (5) has no solution, of the first degree if (5) has a solution, and $p$ is the square of $\mathfrak p$ if (5) has only one solution, that is, if $p$ divides $\Delta$.

The latter agrees with the general theorem of Vol. II, \S\,174.

Using the symbol $(\Delta,p)$ which we introduced in \S\,85, we can also give these theorems the form:

3. If $(\Delta,p)=-1$, then $p$ is indecomposable in $\Omega$.

If $(\Delta,p)=+1$, then $p$ decomposes into two distinct conjugate prime ideals.

If $(\Delta,p)=0$, then $p$ is the square of a prime ideal.

\section*{\S\,93. Representation of numbers as ideal norms.}

A prime number $p$ is the norm of an ideal only when $(\Delta,p)=+1$ or $=0$, but not when $(\Delta,p)=-1$. In the latter case only $p^2$ is a norm, namely that of $\mathfrak p$. In general a positive rational integer $m$ can be represented as a norm in several ways. We shall for the moment denote the number of these representations by $\psi(m)$ and determine it more closely.

First take $m$ to be a prime power $p^k$. If then $(\Delta,p)=+1$ and $p=\mathfrak p\mathfrak p'$, then $p^k$ can be represented in the following way:
\begin{equation}
p^k=\Norm(\mathfrak p^k),\quad
\Norm(\mathfrak p^{k-1}\mathfrak p'),\quad
\Norm(\mathfrak p^{k-2}\mathfrak p'^2),\quad\ldots,\quad
\Norm(\mathfrak p'^k),
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
(\Delta,p)=+1:\qquad \psi(p^k)=k+1.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
But if $(\Delta,p)=-1$, then, when $k$ is even, $p^k=\Norm(\mathfrak p^{k/2})$, whereas if $k$ is odd, $p^k$ cannot be represented as a norm. Thus
\begin{equation}
(\Delta,p)=-1:\qquad \psi(p^k)=0\text{ or }=1,
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
according as $k$ is odd or even. Finally, if $(\Delta,p)=0$, so that $p$ is contained in $\Delta$, then $p=\mathfrak p^2$ and $p^k=\Norm(\mathfrak p^k)$; hence
\begin{equation}
(\Delta,p)=0:\qquad \psi(p^k)=1.
\tag{4}
\end{equation}

These three cases can be combined in one formula. The number $p^k$ has, namely, the following $k+1$ divisors:
\begin{equation}
1,p,p^2,\ldots,p^k.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
Therefore formulas (2), (3), (4), when $m=p^k$ and $n$ runs through the divisors of $m$, give
\begin{equation}
 \psi(m)=\sum_n (\Delta,n).
\tag{6}
\end{equation}

But this expression holds generally. For if $m_1$ and $m_2$ are relatively prime and
\[
 m_1=\Norm(\mathfrak m_1),\qquad m_2=\Norm(\mathfrak m_2),
\]
then
\[
 m=m_1m_2=\Norm(\mathfrak m_1\mathfrak m_2).
\]
Thus from a representation of $m_1,m_2$ as norms one can derive a representation of $m$ as a norm.

Conversely, if
\[
 m_1m_2=\Norm(\mathfrak m)=\mathfrak m\mathfrak m',
\]
where $\mathfrak m,\mathfrak m'$ are conjugate ideals, then, if $m_1$ and $\mathfrak m$ are divisible by a prime ideal $\mathfrak p$, $m_1$ must also be divisible by $\mathfrak p\mathfrak p'$, and so $m_1=\Norm(\mathfrak m_1)$ and likewise $m_2=\Norm(\mathfrak m_2)$.

Accordingly, if $m_1$ and $m_2$ are relatively prime, then
\begin{equation}
 \psi(m_1)\psi(m_2)=\psi(m_1m_2).
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
On the other hand, if $n_1$ and $n_2$ run through the divisors of $m_1$ and $m_2$, then
\[
 \sum(\Delta,n_1)\sum(\Delta,n_2)=\sum(\Delta,n_1n_2),
\]
and with this formula (6) is proved generally. We state this as the theorem:

4. A natural number $m$ can be represented in the quadratic field $\Omega$ with fundamental number $\Delta$ in
\[
 \sum(\Delta,n)
\]
different ways as the norm of an ideal, where $n$ runs through all divisors of $m$.

\section*{\S\,94. The quadratic reciprocity law.}

From the decomposition of prime numbers in the quadratic field, Dedekind derives a new proof of the reciprocity law of quadratic residues.\footnote{Dirichlet-Dedekind, \emph{Zahlentheorie}, 4th ed. (p. 636, note).}

According to Vol. II, \S\,167, 3, the necessary and sufficient condition that the prime ideal $\mathfrak p$ contained in the prime number $p$ (in any field $\Omega$) be of the first degree is that, for every integer $\omega$ of the field $\Omega$,
\begin{equation}
 \omega^p\equiv\omega\pmod{\mathfrak p},
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
and in our case of the quadratic field this reduces to
\begin{equation}
 \theta^p\equiv\theta\pmod{\mathfrak p}.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}

First take $\Delta=-4$. Then $\theta=i=\sqrt{-1}$, and the congruence (2) becomes
\[
 i^{p-1}\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak p}.
\]
Since $(1+i)(1-i)=2$, neither $2$ nor $(1+i)$ can be divisible by $\mathfrak p$ for odd $p$; hence $i^{p-1}=1$, and therefore $p-1$ must be divisible by $4$. Accordingly it follows from \S\,92, 2:

1) The congruence
\[
 x^2\equiv -4\pmod{4p},
\]
or, what is the same,
\[
 x^2\equiv -1\pmod p,
\]
has solutions if and only if $p\equiv1\pmod4$.

Second, take $\Delta=8$. Then $\theta=\sqrt2$, or, if $r=\sqrt i$ is an eighth root of unity,
\[
 \theta=r+r^{-1},
\]
and the condition (2) can be written, by the binomial theorem, as
\[
