 r^p+r^{-p}\equiv r+r^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak p}.
\]
This requires
\[
 (r^p-r)(r^p-r^{-1})r^{-p}\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p},
\]
therefore
\[
 r^p\equiv r\quad\text{or}\quad r^p\equiv r^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak p},
\]
so that
\[
 r^{p\pm1}\equiv1,
 \qquad
 i^{\frac{p\pm1}{2}}-1\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p},
\]
and from this, as above, for an odd $p$,
\[
 p\equiv\pm1\pmod8.
\]

2) The congruence
\[
 x^2\equiv2\pmod p
\]
is therefore solvable if and only if $p\equiv1$ or $p\equiv-1\pmod8$.

Finally let, if $q$ is an odd prime number,
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=\pm q
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
and choose the sign so that $\pm q\equiv1\pmod4$. In this case
\[
 \theta=\frac{-1+\sqrt{\pm q}}{2},
\]
and this expression can be represented by $q$th roots of unity. Let $r$ denote an imaginary $q$th root of unity, and let $a$ run through the quadratic residues and $b$ through the non-residues of $q$. We put, as in Vol. I, \S\,179,
\begin{equation}
 A=\sum_a r^a,
 \qquad
 B=\sum_b r^b,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and have, according to the formulas there, (3), which are derived without use of the reciprocity law,
\begin{equation}
 \theta=A,
 \qquad
 \theta'=B.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
The sign of $\sqrt{\pm q}$ depends on the choice of $r$, but here it does not come into consideration. Now, by the polynomial theorem,
\[
 A^p\equiv\sum r^{pa},
 \qquad
 B^p\equiv\sum r^{pb},
\]
and hence
\[
 A^p\equiv A,
 \qquad
 B^p\equiv B,
 \quad \text{if }\Jac{p}{q}=+1,
\]
and
\[
 A^p\equiv B,
 \qquad
 B^p\equiv A,
 \quad \text{if }\Jac{p}{q}=-1.
\]
Since $A$ is not congruent to $B$ when $p$ is different from $q$, it follows from this and from 2 of the preceding paragraph:

3) The congruence
\[
 x^2\equiv\pm q\pmod p
\]
has solutions if and only if $p$ is a quadratic residue of $q$.

And this is the reciprocity law with its supplementary theorems.



\section*{\S\,95. Equivalent forms and ideals in the quadratic field.}

If the two ideals [\S\,91, (16)]
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak a=(a,a\omega),\qquad \mathfrak a'=(a',a'\omega')
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
are equivalent, then there is a (integral or non-integral) number $\eta$ in $\Omega$ for which $\eta\mathfrak a'=\mathfrak a$, and this number $\eta$ is determined by the ideals $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak a'$ themselves up to a factor which is a numerical unit. Thus there must be four rational integers $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta$ satisfying the condition
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
 \eta a'&=&a(\alpha-\gamma\omega),\\
 \eta a'\omega'&=&a(-\beta+\delta\omega).
\end{array}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
If, for the moment, we denote by $\eta_1,\omega_1,\omega'_1$ the numbers conjugate to $\eta,\omega,\omega'$, then consequently
\[
 a'^2\eta\eta_1
 \begin{vmatrix}1&\omega'\\[0.15em]1&\omega'_1\end{vmatrix}
 =a^2
 \begin{vmatrix}\alpha&-\gamma\\[0.15em]-\beta&\delta\end{vmatrix}
 \begin{vmatrix}1&\omega\\[0.15em]1&\omega_1\end{vmatrix},
\]
that is,
\[
 a'^2\eta\eta_1(\omega'-\omega'_1)=a^2(\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma)(\omega-\omega_1),
\]
and since, by \S\,91, (12),
\[
 a(\omega-\omega_1)=a'(\omega'-\omega'_1)=-\sqrt{\Delta},
\]
it follows that, if
\[
 \varepsilon=\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma
\]
is put,
\begin{equation}
 a\varepsilon=a'\eta\eta_1;
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
and hence, if, as we have already assumed, $a$ and $a'$ are positive and (according to the sharpened notion of equivalence) $\eta\eta_1$ is positive, then $\varepsilon$ is also positive.

But if we interchange $\mathfrak a$ with $\mathfrak a'$, then $\eta$ passes into $1:\eta$, and $\varepsilon$ may pass into $\varepsilon'$. Hence also
\begin{equation}
 a'\varepsilon'=\frac{a}{\eta\eta_1},
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and consequently $\varepsilon\varepsilon'=1$. But since $\varepsilon$ and $\varepsilon'$ are positive rational integers, it follows that $\varepsilon=\varepsilon'=1$. Therefore, by (2),
\begin{equation}
 \omega'=\frac{-\beta+\delta\omega}{\alpha-\gamma\omega},\qquad
 \omega=\frac{\alpha\omega'+\beta}{\gamma\omega'+\delta},\qquad
 \alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=1.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
Now $\omega$ and $\omega'$ are the roots of the two forms
\[
 (a,b,c),\qquad (a',b',c') \qquad [\S\,91,(13)],
\]
and (5) expresses the equivalence of these forms. We therefore have, in agreement with the general theory (\S\,88), the theorem:

5. The quadratic forms belonging to equivalent ideals are equivalent.

From this one recognizes the significance of the sharpened notion of equivalence: for if one also wished to allow negative values of $N(\eta)$, then one could also have $\varepsilon=-1$, and the corresponding forms could be improperly equivalent.

Conversely, let $\varphi=(a,b,c)$ and $\varphi'=(a',b',c')$ be two equivalent forms with positive first coefficients $a,a'$, and let
\begin{equation}
 \omega=\frac{-b+\sqrt\Delta}{2a},\qquad
 \omega'=\frac{-b'+\sqrt\Delta}{2a'}.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
Then there is a linear substitution $\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}$ with determinant $+1$ which carries $(a,b,c)$ into $(a',b',c')$. Then
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
 a'&=&a\alpha^2+b\alpha\gamma+c\gamma^2,\\
 b'&=&2a\alpha\beta+b(\alpha\delta+\beta\gamma)+2c\gamma\delta,\\
 c'&=&a\beta^2+b\beta\delta+c\delta^2,
\end{array}
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \omega=\frac{\alpha\omega'+\beta}{\gamma\omega'+\delta},\qquad
 \omega'=\frac{\delta\omega-\beta}{-\gamma\omega+\alpha},
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
and a small calculation shows that $\omega$ and $\omega'$ are corresponding roots, that is, roots with the same sign of $\sqrt\Delta$.

Accordingly, for the ideals $\mathfrak a,\mathfrak a'$ belonging to $\varphi$ and $\varphi'$ we obtain two basis forms:
\[
 \lambda=a(x-\omega y),
 \qquad
 \lambda'=a'(x'-\omega' y'),
\]
and by (8)
\[
 \lambda=\frac{a[(\delta x-\beta y)+(\gamma x-\alpha y)\omega']}{\gamma\omega'+\delta}.
\]
Thus putting
\[
 x'=\delta x-\beta y,\qquad y'=-\gamma x+\alpha y,
\]
gives
\[
 \lambda=\frac{a}{a'(\gamma\omega'+\delta)}\lambda'.
\]
Since $a:a'(\gamma\omega'+\delta)$ is a number in $\Omega$, it follows that $\mathfrak a$ and $\mathfrak a'$ are equivalent.

Thus again in agreement with the general theorem \S\,88, 4., and at the same time in a more precise determination, we have:

6. If $(a,b,c)$ and $(a',b',c')$ are two equivalent forms with positive first coefficients, and if $\omega,\omega'$ are corresponding roots of these forms, then the ideals $(a,a\omega)$ and $(a',a'\omega')$ are equivalent.

For negative discriminant the first coefficients of equivalent forms always have the same sign, and one considers only the forms in which this sign is positive. For positive discriminant there are forms with positive first coefficients in every form class, and therefore form classes and ideal classes correspond to one another in a one-to-one way.

A basis of the principal ideal $\eta$ is $(\eta,\eta\theta)$ and
\[
 \theta=\frac{\sqrt\Delta}{2},\qquad \theta^2=\frac{\Delta}{4},\qquad \Delta\equiv0\pmod4,
\]
\[
 \theta=\frac{-1+\sqrt\Delta}{2},\qquad \theta^2=\frac{\Delta-1}{4}-\theta,
 \qquad \Delta\equiv1\pmod4.
\]
If $x,y$ are rational integers and
\[
 \eta=x+y\theta,
\]
then this basis is, in the two cases,
\[
 (\alpha_1,\alpha_2)=\left(x+y\theta,\frac{\Delta y}{4}+\theta x\right),
\]
\[
 =\left(x+y\theta,\frac{y(\Delta-1)}{4}+(x-y)\theta\right),
\]
and the substitution determinant $A$ [\S\,87,(1)] is
\[
 x^2-\frac{\Delta}{4}y^2,
 \qquad
 x^2-xy+\frac{1-\Delta}{4}y^2=N(\eta).
\]
The basis $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)$ is therefore positive if the norm of $\eta$ is positive (otherwise $\alpha_1$ would have to be interchanged with $\alpha_2$). From the basis form
\[
 \lambda=\alpha_1t_1+\alpha_2t_2=\eta(t_1+\theta t_2)
\]
one obtains
\[
 N(\lambda)=N(\eta)T,
\]
and accordingly $T$ becomes the principal form
\begin{equation}
 \left(1,0,\frac{\Delta}{4}\right),\qquad
 \left(1,-1,\frac{1-\Delta}{4}\right).
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
If $N(\eta)$ were negative, one would have obtained for $T$ the forms
\[
 \left(-1,0,-\frac{\Delta}{4}\right),\qquad
 \left(-1,1,-\frac{1-\Delta}{4}\right),
\]
and these are equivalent to (9) only if Pell's equation
\[
 t^2-\Delta u^2=-4
\]
is solvable.


\section*{\S\,96. Discriminants of the orders.}

1. \emph{Definition.} If $Q$ is a natural number, then the totality of the integral numbers of the quadratic field $\Omega$ which are congruent, modulo $Q$, to a rational number is called an \emph{order}, and $Q$ is called the conductor of this order. The order with conductor $Q$ is denoted by $[Q]$.

From this definition it follows:

1. All rational integers belong to every order;

2. The sum, difference, and product of two numbers of the order belong to the same order;

that is, within the order addition, subtraction, and multiplication can be performed without restriction, but division cannot in general be so performed.

The totality of all integral numbers of the field $\Omega$ likewise forms an order (of conductor $1$), the principal order, which would therefore be denoted by $[1]$.
\footnote{Dedekind introduced the expression \emph{order} in the general theory of algebraic numbers because, in the special case of the quadratic field, these orders correspond to Gauss's orders of quadratic forms. Hilbert uses for this the expression ``ring'' or ``number ring''. Cf. Dirichlet--Dedekind, \emph{Vorlesungen über Zahlentheorie} (\S\,172 of the third, \S\,170 of the fourth edition). Dedekind: \emph{Über die Anzahl der Idealklassen in den verschiedenen Ordnungen eines endlichen Körpers}, Festschrift zur Säkularfeier von Gauss' Geburtstag, Braunschweig, 1877. Hilbert: \emph{Die Theorie der algebraischen Zahlen}, Bericht der Deutschen Mathematischen Vereinigung, 1894/95, Chap. IX.}

If $\Delta$ is the fundamental number of the field $\Omega$, we put, according as $\Delta$ is even or odd,
\begin{equation}
 \theta_0=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Delta},
 \qquad\hbox{or}\qquad
 \theta_0=\frac{-1+\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}.
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
Then, by \S\,90, every integral number of the field $\Omega$ is representable in the form
\begin{equation}
 \omega=x+y\theta_0,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $x$ and $y$ are integers; and this number is congruent, modulo $Q$, to a rational number $a$ if and only if $y$ is divisible by $Q$, since then
\[
 \frac{x-a+y\theta_0}{Q}
\]
must be an integer. Accordingly all numbers of the order $[Q]$ are contained in the form
\[
 \omega=x+yQ\theta_0,
\]
or, in the two cases distinguished in (1), if
\begin{equation}
 D=Q^2\Delta
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
is put:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rll}
 x-y\dfrac{\sqrt D}{2}, && \Delta\hbox{ even},\\[0.7em]
 x-\dfrac{Q}{2}y+y\dfrac{\sqrt D}{2}, && \Delta\hbox{ odd},\ D\hbox{ even},\\[0.7em]
 x-\dfrac{Q-1}{2}y+y\dfrac{-1+\sqrt D}{2}, && D\hbox{ odd}.
\end{array}
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
Thus, if we put
\begin{equation}
 \theta=\frac{\sqrt D}{2},\quad D\equiv0\pmod4,
 \qquad
 \theta=\frac{-1+\sqrt D}{2},\quad D\equiv1\pmod4,
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
and replace in the two last formulae (4) the arbitrarily integral numbers
\[
 x-\frac{Q}{2}y,
 \qquad x-\frac{Q-1}{2}y
\]
by $x$, we obtain the result:

2. \emph{Theorem.} Every number of the order $[Q]$, and only these, is contained in the form
\begin{equation}
 \omega=x+y\theta,
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
where $x,y$ are arbitrary rational integers.

We call the system
\begin{equation}
 (1,\theta)
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
a basis of the order $[Q]$.

By substituting (5), the numbers (6) may also be represented in the form
\begin{equation}
 \omega=\frac{x+y\sqrt D}{2},
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
where $x,y$ are integers satisfying the condition
\begin{equation}
 x^2-y^2D\equiv0\pmod4.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

The number $D$ is a discriminant whose stem is $\Delta$. We call it the \emph{discriminant of the order} $[Q]$.
